MEMORIAM
THUSNELDE K. DRUMM - Nelde or Tiz, as most people knew her, was a resident of Lunenburg since 1940 and was
known for her style and grace. She was a graduate of Boston University School of Art and taught art in Lunenburg and
surrounding communities and was supervisor of the art department for Lunenburg Public Schools until her retirement
in 1977. She was a practicing silversmith as well as a painter, winning numerous awards. She co-authored the book
"Lunenburg, The Heritage of Turkey Hills" with Margaret Harley, published in 1977. The book is still on sale at the Town
Hall. A ready and capable volunteer for the town, United Parish of Lunenburg, the Fitchburg Art Museum, the
Lunenburg Historical Society and many other committees and organizations. She exemplified the meaning of
commitment to community and love of the arts.
WILLIAM R. PROCTOR SR. - Bill Proctor was born and raised on Clark's Hill, site of Samuel Page's homestead,
the first settler of Lunenburg. Bill was a 1947 graduate of Lunenburg High School. He was a farm boy from a large
family, and though he and his wife Bea traveled all over the world, Lunenburg was where his heart was. Bill was a
familiar face in town. His business, William R. Proctor Inc. recently celebrated 50 years in business. He helped build
many of the roads in town, particularly in the Hickory Hills area. Bill was an avid hunter and fisherman and was a
founding member of the Lunenburg Sportsman Club. Raising a family in Lunenburg, Bill was involved in Little League,
Pop Warner football, and Lunenburg Old Home Days. He was a member of the Lunenburg 250th Anniversary Committee
and twenty-five years later helped organize the Lunenburg 275th Anniversary celebration. His dedication to the town
will be missed.
DONNA SMETANA Donna was a devoted employee of the town's library for over 10 years. She passed away
unexpectedly in July 2006. She worked tirelessly and served in many capacities at the library over the years and her
calm, cheerful presence is sorely missed by staff and patrons allke.
MARGUERITE SOROKA the passing of Marguerite Soroka in December of 2006 was a great loss to the Town of
Lunenburg. She was a Library Trustee and Chairman of the Board for many years as well as an active member of the
United Parish of Lunenburg. Her other town memberships included the Lunenburg Historical Society, the Bookworms,
Lunenburg Women's Club and Smith College Alumni Association. Without her tireless effort the dream of a new
Lunenburg Public Library would never have been realized.
ROBERT L. WARE a long time Sunny Hill Road resident and fourth generation principal In the law firm of Ware
and Ware, Bob served the legal needs of numerous town residents during his 52 years of practice as an estate planning
and conveyance attorney for Fitchburg Savings Bank. By his nature approachable and a conciliator, Bob also served
on the Lunenburg Zoning Board of Appeals from 1964 to 1975 and on the Route 13 Study Committee from 1990 to 1995.
Working with his mother Susan Ware, he handled the legal work for the Lunenburg Memorial Fund, a resource known in
the community for supplementing funding for many town agencies and committees. Bob recently was instrumental in
doing the legal work that redirected 4H funds to the founding of the Chester Mossman Teen Center. Bob's memory,
along with that of Nelde Drumm, was recently honored with the renewal of a neighborhood garden on the corner of
Sunny Hill Road and Elm Street. Bob's lifelong respect for the Lunenburg Police and Fire Departments was made very
personal when they made two rescue calls to his home during the last few months of his life. He was grateful and
relieved to see the familiar faces which gave both personal and professional care during his and his family's time of
need.

===PAGE 10===
BOARD OF SELECTMEN / SEWER COMMISSIONERS
Following the Election In May 2006, Robert H. Bowen was re-elected as Chairman, Thomas A. Alonzo was elected as Vice-
chairman, and Steve deBettencourt was elected as Clerk of the Board. Newest member Paula Bertram took her seat on the Board
along with current member Daniel W. Cronin. Fiscal 2006 marked the first full-year for our new Chief Administrative and Financial
Officer, Daniel M. Keyes. Fiscal 2006 also marked the Initiation of the new Board of Sewer Commissioners. This role in overseeing
the Town's sewer system and enterprise fund was formerly performed by the Board of Selectmen. In order to transition to the new
Board, two members of the Board of Selectmen, Steve deBettencourt and Paula Bertram, were appointed to one year terms on the
Board of Sewer Commissioners. The Commission has been working diligently to establish a sewer department budget, and to
bring the current sewer project to closure.
With the conclusion of the current phase of the sewer project, and pursuant to the vote of Town meeting, the Town has undertaken
the preparation of a Comprehensive Waste-Water Management Plan to assess any future sewering needs for the Town. The much
heralded growth In the Town of Lunenburg Is coming In fits and starts. With approximately 3600 existing residential housing units,
projections of between 1000 to 1200 new units in varlous stages of approval or conceptual proposal raised serious concerns as
the fiscal year began. Many factors, not the least of which has. been the slow down In the residential real estate market, have
tempered the urgency, and should allow us some much needed time to adequately plan for the Impact of such dramatic new
growth on our infrastructure, and on our budget. Under the auspices of the Planning Director, an ad hoc land use committee has
been formed to assist with this necessary planning. Lunenburg has also established "planned production" and approved an
affordable housing "40R" district for the construction of 204 rental units. These are critical tools that will Increase the local control
our land use boards, particularly the Zoning Board of Appeals, will have over the approval of "40B" affordable housing projects In
the future.
Fiscal 2006 found the town settling in to its magnificent new buildings, the public safety complex, the primary school, and the new
library. Work and planning still remains regarding the vacated buildings in the Town Center. The old public safety building is
scheduled to be demolished pursuant to the vote of Town meeting, and the Susan Brown House, which received no blds In a
recent attempt to sell it, will again be put out to bld. Funds have been appropriated to conduct a study with respect to the needs of
Town office space which may utilize some of the other buildings. Town meeting has approved the Community Preservation Act,
which If it passes at the Annual 2007 election, will provide much need funds to accomplish some of these things. The school
department has recently completed a study of its facilities, which may mean that a proposal for the construction of a new high
school may be brought before the voters in the coming years.
As fiscal 2007 comes to a close, we find ourselves at a fiscal crossroads in Lunenburg. We have begun the important task of
planning for our financial future by developing a five-year financial forecast. Like any forecast, it is an evolving document that will
change as each revenue and expenditure assumption is reviewed and modified. Nevertheless, it is a critical tool for our financial
planning and budget analysis. Utilizing that tool, and after careful analysis and Input from the departments, and town committees
such as Capital Planning, the Assessors Office, the Planning Board and the Finance Committee, the budget proposed by the CAFO
calls for a 1.9 million dollar override pursuant to proposition 2 1/2 This figure represents the collective judgment of the revenue
needed to operate the town. If it does not pass, additional cuts will need to be made in all departments to balance the budget. By
a vote of 4-1, the Board of Selectmen decided It was time to put this Important question before the voters.
The Board extends a special "thank you" again this year to the Lunenburg / Townsend Rotary Club for decorating the Gazebo at
Christmas and. the Turkey Hill Gardeners for the seasonal Town Hall decorations and flowers in the upper common. We would
also like to acknowledge the generous donations that were received and utilized so that the Town could continue with the
traditional Band Concerts on the Common and any donations to this cause would be greatly appreciated.
Your Board has enjoyed working in service to the Town, working with each other and with you the citizens. The Selectmen would
also like to acknowledge the Individuals who work so hard for the Town and those who volunteer their time and commitment by
serving on the various Boards, Commissions and Committees within the Town.
Please feel free to direct any calls to our office staff, Daniel M. Keyes, Chief Administrative Financial Officer, Laura Williams, Chief
of Staff / Chief Administrative Assistant or Linda Douglas, Administrative Assistant @ 978-582-4144. If they cannot assist you,
they will forward your call to the appropriate authorities. Our office hours are Monday, Wednesday and Thursday 8:00 AM to 4:00
PM, Tuesday 8:00 AM to 6:30 PM and Friday 8:00 AM to 12:30 PM.

===PAGE 11===
CHIEF ADMINISTRATIVE FINANCIAL OFFICER REPORT
Daniel M. Keyes, Chief Administrative Financial Officer
It has been a full year serving the citizens of Lunenburg as your Chief Administrative Financial Officer and much has been
accomplished yet much more remains. The first and most important Issue was to set an agenda so all departments could work
together for the common good. One of the major concerns facing the department heads and their employees were the unresolved
collective bargaining agreements for the laborers, clerical, fire and police unions. During this past year we have entered into
negotiations and have settled some of the major Issues regarding these contracts, One of those is the skyrocketing cost of health
insurance and we have been able to address that issue to the resolve of both parties.
New growth has become a topic of much concern for the citizens of Town. With the Planning Department seeing an increased
Interest by developers to build between 1000 and 1200 units In Lunenburg the impact on our infrastructure as well our schools,
public safety and daily operations of government will mean an increase In added services for the town and with that come added
costs. These will Include affordable housing (40B) as well as smart growth and the planned housing of the new Emerald Park.
The Meadow Woods Trailer Park issue, which has come under the scrutiny of the Board of Health and the Department of
Environmental Protection (DEP) had to be addressed. The town applied for economic relief to the Department of Agriculture for a
$4,100,000.00 grant/loan with the help of the office of U.S. Senator Edward M. Kennedy and our engineering firm Earth Tech, we
were able to secure the money that is needed to complete this project. Town meeting approved the financing of this project and
would hope that It comes to a successful conclusion over the coming year.
The Department of Environmental Protection (DEP) issued an administrative consent order for areas of concern regarding the
Young's Road Landfill. These included security, surface water, sediment effects and gas monitoring. After the town comes Into
compliance with the (DEP) regulations monitoring of the landfill will continue for another twenty (20) years.
The Storm Water drainage program.was addressed and in the Spring of 2006, our engineering firm along with our Department of
Public Works, began mapping the Town's storm sewer outfalls. Through GPS 376 outfalls and 179 culverts were located,
inspected and mapped. Presently they are developing a plan to Identify and remove suspected Illicit discharges from our storm
sewer system. The Planning Board is reviewing regulatory language and plans to develop by-laws for storm water management.
We brought before town meeting a plan to rehabilitate the old Ritter Library and the Town Hall for use as town offices. We would
then have all town offices In a central location with land use offices In one building and administrative and finance department In
another. This would also allow the school department to utilize the Brooks House for the much needed total administration of the
school department. We also asked that the approval be given to sell the old primary school. This would allow us to apply any
monies from the sale to be applied to our bottom line free cash figure. Both were defeated. We were successful in having town
meeting approve the demolition of the old fire station and the sale of the Susan Brown house (Chief's House).
The Department Heads have worked diligently over the past year on their budgets and their five (5) year projections. Each
department had to deal with the same Issues that all of us as citizens do. In balancing this budget we faced Increases in payroll,
health Insurance, utilities and energy cost due to the increase in the square footage of our new buildings and the increase cost of
OPEC. Some of these costs were unforeseen yet during our budget season meetings each department head worked together to
make our mission successful.
The job of Chief Administrative Financial Officer cannot be done alone. I am only as successful as those around me and I want to
thank the department heads who have made this a successful year. I want to especially thank Karen Brochu, Town Accountant.
Her knowledge of the budget process has been Invaluable and her advice is always welcomed. To members of the staff; Linda
Douglas, Mary Gould and Donna Mayer their help and guidance In the daily activities of the office allow the public to receive
information In a timely and efficient manner. Laura Williams, Chief of Staff, her hard work and dedication not just to this office but
to the Town have not gone unnoticed and this administration and the people of Lunenburg are better for it.
We encourage all residents of Lunenburg to get Involved. Sign up for a committee or commission, call or email our staff with a
suggestion or comment. We know that to better understand local government all it takes is your participation. We also know that
we in government have a responsibility to lead. The Issues we are dealing with are not easy and the answers are complex and
usually expensive. We have a job to do here in Lunenburg. Let's work together. Let's get the job done.

BUILDING DEPARTMENT / ZONING OFFICIAL
The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Town Hall. Office hours for the Building Department are Monday,
Wednesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. until 4:00 P.M., Tuesday 8:00 A.M. until 6:30 P.M. and Friday 8:00 A.M. until 12:30 P.M. Michael
J. Sauvageau serves as the Building Commissioner and Lisa Normandin is the Administrative Assistant. Please call to schedule
an inspection or to make appointments.
Building permits for new single-family dwelling units has slowed considerably due to the housing market. Additions and other
renovations such as decks, roofing and siding permits remained steady. The number of building permits Issued in 2006 was
actually slightly higher than 2005. As the housing market picks up, the number of building permits should Increase with close to
one thousand housing units
The 7th Edition of the Massachusetts State Building Code CMR 780 should be In effect by May 2007. Individuals and contractors
seeking permits will have six months from the edification date of the new residential code to choose the existing 6th Edition or the
new 7th Edition for construction.
Carbon monoxide detectors are now required in every home existing as well as new construction.
Homeowners should be aware that Inflatable swimming pools do not meet code compliance and should contact our office to
determine what needs to be done for compliance.
Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations who bid or perform
residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions etc.) for work exceeding $1000.00 be registered with the
Commonwealth. This office will enforce this provision, however you should ask your contractor about his/her registration. Please
contact this office with any questions about this requirement or call for any information regarding zoning requirements or building
code issues.
CONSERVATION COMMISSION
The Lunenburg Conservation Commission Is an appointed board comprised of seven members who volunteer their time and
expertise to protect the wetlands, water related resources and adjoining land areas in the Town of Lunenburg. They are
responsible for upholding the Massachusetts Wetlands Protection Act, the Lunenburg Wetland Protection By-law, the
management and acquisition of conservation land and assisting the public with ecological, wetland Issues and concerns. The
Commission also assists applicants In filing the proper forms, holding public hearings, arranging site visits and making decisions
it deems necessary regarding the protection of wetlands.
The Conservation Commission has approximately one thousand eight hundred and fifty acres under its Jurisdiction at this time.
Several parcels of land are accessible to the public and are used by many people for hiking. In the coming year the Commission
plans to create a listing of all the property, a description of the area, accessibility, trails and possibly maps of the Individual sites
showing trails and points of interest.
The year 2006 was a very active year for the Commission. Filings and public hearings were held on four Abbreviated Notice of
Resource Delineation, forty-seven Notice of Intent and thirteen Request for Determination of Applicability. Fees received by the
town for these hearings were eighteen thousand two hundred and thirty six dollars.
The house and conservation land on Hollis Road now has a Resident Caretaker/Custodian. We were fortunate to find someone
who is eager to take on the task of conservation property maintenance and upgrading the house.
The Conservation Office is located on the second floor of Town Hall, next to the stage. Office hours. are Monday through
Thursday, 7:30 AM - 3:00 PM, also Tuesday 4:30 PM 6:30 PM. Hearings are held the first and third Wednesdays of every month;
the first Wednesday is at the Brooks' House and the third Wednesday is at Town Hall.
Commission members are: Rob Verge, Chairman, J.J. Cote, Vice Chairman, John Lattanzi, Jack Byrne, Richard Bursch, Kenneth
Jones and Gregory Roy. Matt Marro, Conservation Agent, continues to provide technical support to the Commission, other Town
Boards and the general public. Jan Carrier is the Administrative Assistant.

"This is a Cemetery. lives are commemorated - deaths are recorded - families are reunited - memories are made tangible - and
love is undisguised. This is a cemetery. Communities accord respect, families bestow reverence, historians seek information and
our heritage is thereby enriched. Testimonies of devotion, pride and remembrance are craved In stone to pay warm tribute to
accomplishments and to the life - not death of a loved one. The cemetery Is homeland for family memorials that are a sustaining
source of comfort to the living. A cemetery is a history of people - a perpetual record of yesterday and a sanctuary of peace and
quiet today. A cemetery exists because every life is worth loving and remembering - always" Mary Lou Brannon

BOARD OF HEALTH
Revised Title V regulations went into effect in the Spring of 2006. As anticipated, local Boards of Health were given even more
duties In the review and approval of projects as well as increased enforcement powers. As would be expected, additional
responsibilities and duties only Increase the amount of work to be done during a static period of time. Board hours will increase
by 2 hours per week In the 2008 fiscal year, which will be of some help.
We have provided a great deal of Information from our databases to Wright-Pierce Inc., the engineering firm retained by the Town
to perform the Comprehensive Wastewater Management Plan, and have met with them several times with comments and
clarifications of the Initial phase of that project. We will continue to take part In this very important project to Insure that when
complete, It will reflect a true and accurate assessment of the Town.
Homeland Security planning Is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other Town departments and regional entities to
develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but other towns In our region. Last years' Town Meeting, passed an article which allows the
Board of Health to enter Into Mutual Aid agreements with neighboring towns, much as our Fire Department has with our neighbors.
While no agreements have been made to this point, it is a valuable tool which can be put Into place more quickly if the need arises.
As always, the Board of Health, through the NABH, continues to provide many, many clinics for both the young and seniors In our
Community. Through free dental clinics in the schools, we help ensure healthy teeth in our youngsters. If you have children,
please take advantage of this service.
Throughout the year, numerous clinics are held at various places within the town. Please contact the Board office at 582-4135 if
you would like further information.
NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the local Board of Health In meeting the challenges of environmental and
public health needs in Lunenburg. In addition to the day to day public health work conducted for Lunenburg we also provide the
following services. Maintaining Nashoba's internet web site to provide information for the public. (See nashoba.org)
Through our involvement In the Bioterrorlsm Regional Coalition we are keeping the Lunenburg Board of Health up-to date on
matters of emergency preparedness planning. We are currently working on the Emergency Dispensing Site plan for
Lunenburg.
Response to the West Nile Encephalitis(WNV) surveillance effort through coordination with MDPH, through the collection of
suspected bird samples, submittal to the MDPH lab, and follow-up when the lab results were obtained.
Response to health related Impacts of beavers through coordination with the local Board of Health In the issuance of the
necessary permits.
Response to state mandated changes in regulations requiring more frequent beach sampling through sample collection,
submittal for analysis, and follow-up when results were obtained.
Increased use of the Housing Court to affect compliance with State Sanitary and Environmental Codes.
We look forward to continuing our work with Lunenburg's Board of Health. Included in the day-to-day work of Nashoba in 2006 were the
following:
Through membership in the Association Lunenburg benefited from the services of Nashoba staff Including: Registered
Sanitarians, Certified Health Officers, Registered Nurses, Nutritionists, Registered Physical & Occupational Therapists,
Licensed Social Workers, Certified Home Health Aides, and Registered Dental Hygienists
Nashoba Nursing Service is the regional vaccine depot for MDPH. As such It is a valuable resource for the regular
distribution of vaccines to physicians in the area, provides regular public Immunization clinics, and is positioned and
equipped to provide for larger scale Immunizations should MDPH require them.
Provided health education programs in collaboration with the Lunenburg Council on Aging.
Collaborated with Montachusett Home Care around elders at risk and other safety issues.
Responded to and assessed mental health concerns through crisis Intervention and teamwork with other community
resources Including the Lipton Center.
Reviewed 78 Title 5 state mandated private Septic System Inspections for Lunenburg Board of Health. Received, reviewed,
and filed these state mandated (but unfunded) Title 5 inspections. Corresponded with Inspectors regarding deficiencles;
referred deficient inspections to Lunenburg Board of Health for enforcement action.

Environmental Information Responses Lunenburg Office (days)

The Nashoba sanitarian Is generally scheduled to be available for the public twice a week on Monday and Wednesday

Food Service Licenses & Inspections
53
Nashoba annually malls out and receives application from both restaurants and retail food businesses. Licenses are
renewed for existing facilities. Plans are submitted and reviewed prior to Initial licensing. Most licensees are Inspected
at a minimum twice a year. Where deficiencies are found, a re-inspection is scheduled to Insure compliance. When a
complaint from the public is received an Inspection is also, conducted. During this inspection health factors of food
handlers is also Investigated, and where appropriate medical consultation and laboratory testing may be required.

20

suspected.

unsanltary conditions or pollution events are Investigated.

coordinated with the applicant's engineer.

conducted by the applicant's engineer which serve as the basis of the design of the septic system.

routed to the Nashoba sanitarian for review.

Communicable Disease
Nashoba's Nursing Service & Environmental Health Department work together to meet the local Board of Health's responsibilities
under the law to do the following:
Investigate and control the spread of communicable diseases within Lunenburg (MGL Chapter 111, Section 6, 7 & 92-116).

public health".
Receive reports and undertake follow-up as necessary regarding certain food borne and waterborne diseases and diseases
being monitored by the MDPH.
Receive reports, investigate and conduct follow-up on all incidences involving positive rabies results.

CAPITAL PLANNING
The Capital Planning Committee, as noted in past years, follows the Charter's Direction to present FY plans and Capital Program
expenditures. The Committee's capital budget is presented through the Chief Administrative and Financial Officer to the Board of
Selectmen and the Finance Committee. The final plan is voted at Town Meeting.
The FY yearly plans determine the needs of the governmental departments for the coming year. The FY yearly plan includes the
needs of the service departments, the DPW, Fire and Police. It further Includes the schools, library, technology, land use
departments and the chief operating offices. The Capital Program Plan deplcts the long range needs of the Town such new
buildings and/or major building renovations.
The FY'07 yearly plan, as determined by the Capital Planning Committee from the requests of all departments, was not passed in
May. 2006. The plan was drastically cut and re-presented at the December 2006 Special Town Meeting.
The Special Town Meeting passed the following:
Police Department at Two (2) Police Cruisers $73,000.00
Fire Department . Turn Out Gear $10,000.00
Fire Department . Fire Hose $15,000.00
DPW Department - 6 wheel Dump Truck w/plow/sander $55,000.00
School Department - ES Alarm/Security/Door Replacement $46,100.00
Technology Department - Annual replacement of Town Technology $50,900.00
The recommendation of the CPC for the FY'07 plans was $630,086.00. The criteria of "Ability to Fund" required the CPC to
drastically reduce the plan to $370,761.00. In FY'07 and as noted above, the further reduced bottom line of $250,000.00 was
approved. Therefore the above expenditures did not come close to the amount to meet the needs.
Each request Is analyzed according to the Criteria, which is Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis, Cost Effectiveness and Ability to Fund.
Each request is reviewed with the Department Heads and managed by a determination of the criteria along with urgency of the
needed Item. The requested expenditures are the tools that provide services of safety and protection, the management of
government and the means to keep facilities and grounds in good condition. The CPC works through the criteria effectively until
it comes to "Ability to Fund."
The Committee, as in FY'07, has to recommend on the basis of deferral. Deferral brings consequences such as deficient capital
items today, backlogged capital items, Inflation of cost, poorly maintained Infrastructure, Increase in annual operating expenses
and not least of all, lesser service to the public.
The Capital Program was not funded in FY'07. Past Capital Program articles reports show that the heating system for the
elementary /high schools has been reimbursed to the town. Monies expended for the fire truck was $298,000. Total payments for
the Library and Public Safety Buildings was $641,002 and the first payment reimbursement for the primary school was $513,448.
Each year, we drag out the "Big Man-Little Man" analogy. It is a comparison of the Omnibus Budget (Operating) and the Capital
Budget. This comparison is not a negative against operating needs but a plea for the Town to realize that Capital upkeep must
have the support of a continuous balance In Capital Spending. Continuous Balance of Capital Expenditures meets the criteria of
Safety, Need and Cost Effectiveness. Balance can not be obtained when the capital needs is continuously deferred.
Marion Benson, Chair, Planning Board Representative
Steven deBettencourt, Board of Selectmen
Milree Keeling, School Committee
David Matthews, Finance Committee
Elaine Murphy, Citizen's Representative

CENTRAL MASSACHUSETTS MOSQUITO CONTROL PROJECT
The Central Massachusetts Mosquito Control Project (the Project) currently provides its services to 36 cities and towns throughout
Middlesex and Worcester Countles. The Project's headquarters is located at 111 Otls Street, Northboro, MA. Tours of the
headquarters or visits to field work sites may be arranged by calling the office In advance. Please call (508) 393-3055 during
business hours for more information. The Project practices Integrated Mosquito Management (IMM), blending state of the art
methods and techniques with expertise, experience, and scientific research to provide our member communities with
environmentally sound and cost effective mosquito control.
During 2006 the Project received eight thousand and fifty five (8,455) requests for service from town residents and officials. A total
of over six thousand (6,000) pounds of Bti (Bacillus thuringlensis israelensis} was applied by helicopter in 2 towns, Chelmsford
& Billerica, and five thousand, nine hundred and fifty nine (5,959) pounds by hand throughout our service area were applied to area
wetlands to reduce the emergence of adult mosquitoes. This represents over one thousand, one hundred and ninety one (1,191)
acres of wetland that was treated with this mosquito-specific bacterlum, significantly reducing adult mosquito populations in these
areas. Thirty three thousand, nine hundred and eighteen (33,918) catch basins were treated with larvicidal product to control the
mosqultoes that seek out these cool dark wet areas to breed, including the Culex mosquito, a major target for West Nile Virus
transmission. Seven thousand, three hundred and ninety three (7,393) culverts were cleaned in an attempt to eliminate
unnecessary standing water and reduce mosquito breeding. This work was done in conjunction with cleaning, clearing, and
digging of one hundred and seventy six thousand, one hundred and eighty one (176,181) feet of streams, brooks and ditches. This
represents over thirty three.(33) miles of waterways which were cleaned and Improved by Project personnel In 2006.
The Mosquito Awareness Program which we offer to elementary schools and other civic organizations In our district has become
very popular. Project staff meets with students, teachers or concerned residents to discuss mosquito biology, mosquito habitat,
and control procedures. Much of the presentation is directed towards what children and their families can do to prevent
mosquitoes from breeding around their homes. Slides, videos, coloring books and other handouts make this an interesting
program. This program Is tallored to meet the needs of the specific audience. Two thousand, two hundred and fifty (2,250)
students attended these programs.
As part of our effort to reduce the need for pesticides we continue to expand our wetlands restoration program. By cleaning
clogged and overgrown waterways, mosquito breeding can be reduced and drainage areas are restored to historic conditions.
Btl mosquito larvicide is used to treat areas where mosquito larvae are found. We routinely check known breeding sites kept In
out database, but also encourage the public to notify us of any areas they suspect could breed mosquitoes. Our field crews will
Investigate all such requests and treat the area only If surveillance gathered at the time shows an imminent threat of mosquito
emergence.
Our goal is to manage all mosquito problems with education, wetlands restoration or larviciding, but we recognize that there are
times when adult mosqulto spraying is the only viable solution. In such cases specific areas are treated with either hand-held or
pickup truck mounted sprayers if surveillance gathered at the time exceeds a pre-determined threshold to warrant an application.
This program is offered on a request-only basis, and the exclusion process allows residents and/or town officials to exclude areas
under their control from this or any part of our program.
The Project's surveillance program monitors adult mosquito and larval population density, and is the backbone for prescribing
various control techniques. Specialized mosquito traps are deployed throughout the Project's service area to sample for
mosquitoes that may be transmitting mosquito-borne diseases. In conjunction with the Mass. Dept. of Public Health we sample in
areas suspected of harboring WNV and other viruses. One thousand, five hundred and twenty eight (1,528) pools (collections) of
mosquitoes totaling thirty nine thousand, five hundred and six (39,506) specimens were tested for mosquito-borne viruses this
year. One (1) pool of Cs. morisitans in Leominster was confirmed to be infected with the EEE virus late in the season. Two (2)
pools of West Nile Virus were confirmed, both in Westborough in the Culex species. Two (2) human cases were identified with
WNV, one in Billerica and one in Webster. No human cases of EEE were Identified, and there were no cases of WNV or EEE in
horses in our service area.
Educational pamphlets are available to anyone interested in learning about mosquito control and the services provided by the
Project, and these Items are routinely stocked in member Town/City Halls and libraries. Display boards with Information on our
program are rotated through area Town Halls throughout the year. We also have a website, www.cmmcp.org that has extensive
information on mosquito biology, our control procedures, etc. This website has become a model for other Mosquito Projects and
has been widely used throughout our service area and beyond.
We would like to thank you for your support during 2006 and we look forward to helping you and your community with Its
mosquito problems in 2007 and beyond.

===PAGE 19===
COUNCIL ON AGING
Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts General Law Chapter 40 Section 8B to include setting of local policy for
the administration of elder programs or services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder
Issues.
In FY'06 the Council on Aging board members were: Shella Lumi, Chairperson; Roland LeBlanc, Vice Chair; Theresa Longsjo
Brackett, Robert Fickett, Emily Foster, Isabelle Gallagher, Edith Gay, Barbara Raboin, Rennie Shattuck and Judith Tarbell. The
board accepted the resignation of long-time member Edith Carey. Gloria O'Brien filled her position.
The Council on Aging staff consisted of Jeanne DeBruin, Director; Susan Doherty, Principal Clerk; Faith Anderson, Outreach
Coordinator; and Ruth Swiecicki, Meal Site Manager.
The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. The Friends of the Eagle House Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group Is
dedicated to assisting the Council on Aging In their mission to provide a place for elders to maintain a healthy, active and
meaningful lifestyle. This past year the Friends contributed approximately $7200.00 to supplement the Town's budget, by
providing for the costs for all the educational and recreational programs, the Montachusett Opportunity. Council donation of
$900.00 to help fund the Nutrition Program, the Memorial Garden maintenance, Volunteer Recognition Events, newsletter mailing
costs and other miscellaneous items. Without their assistance we could not accomplish all that we do. The center also receives
funds from the state Formula Grant and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
Our volunteers also contribute to the success of the center. Seventy-three volunteers gave 3685 hours to the Eagle House. The In-
Kind service value for the volunteer hours was worth $29,156.00. The federal census of year 2000, upon which our state funding is
based, listed Lunenburg's elder population at 1505. According to Town records, by the end of 2007, there will be 2053 elders who
are age 60 or older In Lunenburg. We hope to continue to assist our growing elder population so that they can choose to remain in
their homes for as long as possible. Our Lunenburg elders enrich the life of our community. They continue to volunteer in many
areas of Town life. Educational and recreational needs are part of maintaining a productive life, along with home- based programs,
which the Outreach Coordinator facilitates.
Many citizens of our town and other towns compliment us on the multi-faceted programs our director plans. A few of the topics
and services covered were: Medicare Part D Programs, Estate Planning, Food Stamps, Legal Blindness, Pain Initiative (a state-
wide program) Fall Prevention, Meet the Tax Assessor, Public Safety Day, Tea for Good Health, Conversation Before a Crisis,
Diabetic Cooking, Poetry Writing, Talking Book Program, Older Wiser Driver, Fuel Assistance, Meet the CAFO. The Outreach
coordinator planned our first daylong Health Fair and arranged for the Veteran's Homestead Mobile Medical Unit to visit our center.
Unfortunately our challenge to (1) Increase our much-needed Outreach Coordinator's hours and (2) to provide for additional off-
street parking was not met. It is the COA's plan to work toward that effort this coming year. Outreach often means Intervention,
which then provides assistance and referrals at no cost to our families. All this is possible through an established and stable
outreach program. This position Is critical to our social service operation. Many elders and their family members request
assistance with eligibility for transportation, health insurance Issues, legal aid socialization needs' and varlous means tested
programs. Last year there was a 13% Increase In the number of clients served and a 36% increase in the number of services
rendered. Our population is aging and will continue to do so. Senior centers and Councils on Aging are one of the highest
identified sources of information for elders in Massachusetts. We are not just recreational centers; we are a multi-service resource
and referral provider.
The FY'06 report to the state shows we provided 31,534 units of service to 1087 elders. Of these services 21,934 units were for
Wellness and Support. Of these services 40% were given to persons 75 years of age or older. in addition 995 separate units were
given to non-elders.
How fortunate we are to be able to have elders continue to interact with the youth of our community. Intergenerational activities
such as the letter writing Pen Pal Program and the High School WWII Interviews bring enrichment to both generations.
It Is the mission of the Council on Aging to advocate for improved quality of life for all seniors through supportive services,
programs and éducation. Anyone who would like to volunteer his or her services please call the senior center at (978) 582-4166.

DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
John M. Rodriquenz, DPW Director
"The mission of the Department of Public Works Is to protect, preserve and Improve the Town's Infrastructure
and related assets in a manner which meets and enhances the current and-future social and economic needs of
the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality. environment for the Town's citizens and visitors, and
to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means of disposing of solld waste and waste water"
The Department of Public Works combines the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions into one department
organizing personnel and equipment to serve the residents of our community. Lunenburg continues to be faced with state
mandated compliance associated with EPA regulations for storm water management and landfill monitoring. These requirements
place an added burden on public works personnel and increase budget amounts particularly as engineering consultants are
required. A hazardous waste management program Inclusive of the vehicle maintenance / fueling facility, and a town wide phase II
separate storm system require Identification, mapping, permitting, and a continued plan for Improvements and increased public
awareness.
The following is a breakdown of line item allocations and expenditures:
Labor and Overtime Wages
The amount of $387,639.00 was budgeted for highway division personnel payroll and $3,000.00 for overtime (excluding winter
emergencies). The actual amounts expended from these lines totaled $331,425.94 from payroll and $3,429.43 from overtime.
Positions within the highway division were left unfilled due to a spending / hiring freeze. These funds were transferred to cover
overages in other lines throughout the course of the fiscal year.
General Highway Maintenance
The cost of paving and patching materials, drainage supplies, roadside mowing, shoulder repair, and street sweeping are funded
through this line. The purchase of tools, small equipment items, and the outsourcing of tasks beyond the capabilities of the
department are also funded through this account. $73,000.00 was the Initial allocation and an amount of $65,687.88 was spent.
$25,000.00 of the total allocation was dedicated to state mandated storm water management.
Traffic Signs
Utility charges to operate electronic signalization at the Intersection of Whalom Road and Route 13 and caution signs at school
zones are funded through this Item. Standardized street signs, stop signs etc. are purchased with these funds as well as roadway
striping, stop bars and crosswalks. An amount of $6,500.75 was charged against a budget amount of $5,950.00 requiring an Intra-
budget transfer of $550.75. The lack of adequate funding does not allow for town wide line painting that historically had been a
yearly task.
Facilities Maintenance
The Department of Public Works is responsible for the care and maintenance of all town buildings and properties (excluding those
of the School Dept.) $54,300.00 was the original amount budgeted (excluding salaries). An additional $5,000.00 was added to this
line thru the 5/6/2006 Special Town Meeting, $11,137.72 was transferred from the reserve fund and $5,000.00 was transferred thru
an intra-budget transfer. Actual expenditures were $73,456.61 with over $65,000.00 directed to preventive maintenance alone.
Appropriate staffing and funding for the care of our town buildings new and old is grossly Inadequate.
Town Highway Garage
The highway division/vehicle maintenance facility at 520 Chase Road serves as the central location for the Public Works
Department. Costs to operate and maintain the physical plant and offices: utilities, waste removal, janitorial supplies, postage,
and office equipment totaled $14,722.22. An Intra-budget transfer was made to supplement the original allocation of $13,513.00.
Vehicle Maintenance
This division within the Department of Public Works is responsible for the repair and fueling of every town vehicle. High
performance public safety vehicles, police cruisers, fire apparatus, school, park, and cemetery vehicles, as well as over twenty
pleces of equipment within the highway division are serviced through this program. The department is unable to keep up with
technological advancements in the new vehicles and as a result there is a greater need to out source repairs. The entire allocation
of $91,944.00 was expended. Overruns Incurred by the Police, Fire and School Departments were covered both by reserve fund
transfers ($5,000.00 for the Police and $2,500.00 for the Fire Dept.) and by Intra-budget transfers. The total expenditures for
Vehicle Maintenance were $120,138.43. The cost of gasoline, diesel fuel and all petroleum based products has skyrocketed. It Is
safe to assume that these increases will continue. It becomes increasingly difficult to budget accurately given the volatility of the
petroleum industry. We must however prepare in order to maintain the integrity of adequate public safety response.

===PAGE 21===
safe to assume that these increases will continue. It becomes increasingly difficult to budget accurately given the volatility of the
petroleum Industry. We must however prepare In order to maintain the Integrity of adequate public safety response.
Tree Removal
As tree warden, the public works director oversees all tree work within the town right of way. The budgeted amount within this line
remained at $7,000.00 for FY'06. Many of our two hundred lane miles are lined with mature trees which regularly require emergency
removal or trimming $10,468.00 was expended, again covered by an Intra-budget transfer. Drastic cuts In this line have caused this
department to eliminate any preventative maintenance program.
Snow Removal
Clearing the roadways of Ice and snow Is the primary function of the DPW throughout the winter season. Road sand, processed
by town personnel, is hauled to the highway garage during summer months and mixed with salt to treat roadways during Ice and /
or snow events. The salt Is purchased through a cooperative of fourteen communities to Insure the lowest possible price per ton
delivered. As is often the case this account ran in a deficit. $135,000.00 was budgeted, $190,957.54 was spent. The rise In cost of
fuel, materials, repairs and labor would suggest a readjustment to a more realistic allocation.
Sewer Construction
The Massachusetts Highway Department in conjunction. with the Lunenburg department of public works reconstructed the
sidewalks and repaved the entire length of Rt. 2A / Massachusetts Avenue from Sunnyhill Road to Arbor Street as the final
remediation of Contract II sewer installation.
As always, I am grateful for the continued support and patience of our citizens and fellow town employees as we proceed through
these difficult times.
PARK DEPARTMENT
The summer basketball program continues to be ably directed by Bob Bourque. Bob's fourth year as a director was again a
tremendous success. Under Bob's expert guidance the program has evolved into three separate leagues. The varsity boy's
league has been Joined by a varsity girl's league under the lights along with a junior varsity boy's league. Six games a might are
played four nights a week on adjoining courts under the lights at Wallls Park. The league begins the last week of school and
crowns three champions around August 1st,
A new storage shed/snack bar was erected In early summer at Wallis Park. The Park Department would like to thank the
Lunenburg Memorial Fund along with Frank Priest Roofing, Perspective Development Company, Proctor Landscaping and
Masonry Supply and numerous other Individuals for their generous donations and time.
A temporary Skateboard Park was developed in the fall at Marshall Park. Many thanks to P. J. Keating Company for the asphalt
and grading work they donated. This park will be in use until a more permanent location can be found.
The Park Department sponsored Winter Junior Basketball program and has been expanded to Include children in grades 1 thru 8.
Over 400 children participate on 43 private or corporate sponsored teams. The program Is divided into 8 different recreational
leagues. The 1st and 2nd grade, 3rd and 4th grade, 5ᵗʰ and 6th grade along with the 7th and 8ᵗʰ grade players are divided into boys
and girls leagues crowning 8 champions. The Park Department also sponsors 9 travel teams representing boys and girls in grades
4 - 8. Many people have given their time to ensure this programs success. We'd like to give special thanks to Com and Sue
Marrabello, Peter Patno, Kevin O'Connell, Tara Worthley, Gary McMaster, Bryan Bradley and Vicki Downey. We'd be remiss if we
didn't thank program director Cliff Worthley for his tireless years of continued service to the young people of Lunenburg.
The Town Beach and staff again enjoyed another successful season. Gen Nass and her staff provide swim lessons, lifeguard
training and many hours of professional lifeguard duty. Many families continue to use this facility to "Beat the Heat" in June, July
and August.
The Lunenburg Park Department would like to note their appreciation to the Town for approving the article that will allow the
Lunenburg Track and Field Association to complete the track and field project situated at Lunenburg High School.
The Park Department has one full time employee maintenance supervisor Tom Leppala. Tom has done an outstanding job for the
past 23 years and we believe Is a tremendous asset to the Town.

LUNENBURG PUBLIC LIBRARY
The year 2006 saw many changes for the newly opened Lunenburg Public Library. Most were happy, such as the tremendous
increase In people using our new building - circulation Increased over 30% and we have 50 people an hour using the library on
weekdays and more than 70 on Saturdays. It has been fantastic discovering how hugely popular our meeting rooms have become
for hosting local meetings and special events.
Unfortunately, 2006 was also filled with sorrow. We unexpectedly lost our dear beloved staff member, Donna Smetana last July.
Her death left a great emptiness In the heart of our library family. Donna worked at the library for over 10 years in many capacities
and Is missed by patrons as well as our staff. Another loss to our library family was that of Marguerite Soroka. Marguerite passed
away In December. She was a Library Trustee and Chairman of the Board for many years. Marguerite was Influential In helping to
build our beautiful new library and was a mainstay of the Lunenburg community for many years. We will all miss her bright light
and shining optimism.
We suffered another loss with the retirement of our Director, Susan Tallman in November. Susan was our director for 17 years and
led the library through many changes in our town. Through her vision and foresight, she was Instrumental in making the dream of
a new library a reality. She was a wonderful leader and we miss her dearly. We have been blessed with our new staff members
Laurie Kelly and Lunenburg High School senior, Patrick Fitzgerald. They have helped to fill in the gap left by these many changes.
The library has undergone more than-just a physical metamorphosis. We have grown to become a community center for the town.
Our meeting rooms were booked over 350 times during our first year and that doesn't Include our study rooms which have a
waiting list practically every afternoon for the after school crowd. Many people come in to use our high speed Internet and Wifi
services. More than 5000 people have Lunenburg Public Library cards and many more use library cards from surrounding towns.
Our Interlibrary loans have passed the 1000 mark each month - more than 1000 items come in and go out of our library to our
patrons and to other libraries in our region - a service we provide free for anyone with a card.
Our Friends group, who are such devoted, dedicated people, has increased in membership to become the 5th highest In the state -
surpassing even the Boston Public Libraryl Our Museum Pass program continues to be popular and many more programs are
now possible with our larger facility and blgger parking lot. This has made an unbellevable differencel
Volunteers are such an Important part of our library that we could not function nearly as well without all of their incredible
donations of time and energy. We have more than a dozen volunteers, who donate upwards of 50 hours each week towards
keeping the library running smoothly and efficiently. They are an incredible asset to our town and we cannot thank them enough
for all of their hard work.
Although 2006 was a year of great sadness for us, it was also a time of tremendous growth and change. We are very proud to be
such an integral part of the town and to serve S0 much of its-ever-growing and ever-changing population.

PLANNING BOARD
The Planning Board membership has two new members, Joanna Bllotta and Robert Salla. They were appointed to fulfill the terms
of Deborah Seeley and Gary Prime until the next election. Deborah Seeley was a long term member and Is greatly missed for her
questioning deliberations and thoughtful decisions. Gary Prime was on the Board for a short period of time and left for health
reasons. The present officers are Chair, Lynn Sallee, Vice Chair, Emerick Bakaysa and Clerk, James Halloran. Marlon Benson
serves as Planning Director.
In review of the year, the Board approved 17 ANR plans (approval for frontage lots on a Town Approved Way). The Board has
Issued approvals for multi-developments which require Special Permits and/or Development Plan Review Approvals. Two major
projects that had thorough and full reviews are Tri-Town Landing, Youngs Road, a Chapter 40R submittal and Emerald Place at
Lake Whalom, Lakefront, a Zoning Bylaw by Right Project. The Board has had closure on the Harley House Project,
Massachusetts Avenue, to operate as an Inn; Lena Lane, Lancaster Avenue/Gibson Street, a 4 unit subdivision has been approved
for construction; Stone Farm Estates, Massachusetts Avenue; White Tall Crossing, Burrage Street; and Whites Woods, White
Street and Massachusetts Avenue are still in construction. Two commercial establishments were permitted; Trudie's Treats,
Lancaster Avenue and First Choice Limousine, Chase Road. Two applications for telecommunication towers were received, one
for 621 Chase Road and one for 270 Electric Avenue. Whalom Storage's original plan for 80,000 sq ft of self-storage, Electric
Avenue, was in review and withdrawn. A new revised plan was submitted on January 25, 2007. Essent Health Care,
Massachusetts Avenue, submitted an application for expansion In December.
The philosophy of Planning in this Community is to grow slowly with matching infrastructure. The Planning Board has
encouraged the village type of development; however, by right and mandated development, larger projects have been submitted.
Also, developers have placed conceptual proposals on the table which gives the perception of rapid growth. These conceptuals
will not be given full review until a statutory submittal is before the Board.
The issue of mandated growth was partly addressed with the Smart Growth Bylaw. The 40R project at Tri-Town Landing, which is
in the final stages before construction, will assist In meeting the mandated quota set forth by the State Inventory. Further, Planned
Production approval by the State for the Town will be effective in managing the timing of any future 40B projects once the Town
meets a percentlie of the State quota. This Is a process that gives the Town the opportunity to manage a timeline in this type of
development.
At the December Special Town Meeting, a Bylaw was passed that mandates that any conventional development must set aside a
percentage of structures to meet the affordable criteria. This process will help keep L'unenburg's standing on the top percentile of
the State Inventory. This allows the Town to be in charge of the rate of mandated growth. Conventional growth has not surpassed
the criteria set forth in the Bylaw.
The Planning Board's desire is to establish a strong land use department Ad Hoc Committee. The group has only met a few times.
The Planning Department is presently preparing a housing market study in the area, which will also reflect the State's pursuit of
small residential units, Including rentals, to present to the Ad Hoc Committee.
The Board has commented on the first draft report of the CWMP and has noted Input to the need of balancing in residential sewer
construction, while at the same time Investing in sewer construction that will assist commercial and industrial growth. Year 2006
has not been a year of growth in regards to residential sales and commercial/industrial enterprises.
Planning Board members serve as representatives on other Committees. James Halloran serves as the Planning representative to
the Montachusetts Regional Planning Commission and the School Feasibility Study; Robert Salla, as representative to the
Montachusetts Joint Transportation Commission; Emerick Bakaysa is the Planning Board representative to the Charter Review
Commission and Joanna Bllotta is the representative to Lake Shirley's 319 Study. The Board has appointed Marion Benson,
Planning Director to represent the Board on the Capital Planning Committee.
Lynn Sallee, Chair
Emerick Bakaysa, Vice Chair
James Halloran, Clerk
Robert Salla, Member
Joanna Bilotta, Member
Marlon M. Benson, Planning Director

FIRE DEPARTMENT
This year the Fire Department has responded to Increased calls for service as well as multiple requests for plans review of new
construction. This will continue to be the trend as the town changes and grows over the next few years. As these demands
Increase, so will the need to increase our staffing of both call and career firefighters. The Increased demand for service also
impacts our need for supplies and funding for vehicle maintenance as our vehicles are on the road more frequently.
Last year the town's first ladder truck was commissioned into service. This year, I want you to know that the ladder truck has and
will continue to play a vital role in the preservation of life and property. The purchase of the ladder truck has, without question,
enhanced fire ground safety and allowed us to perform quicker and safer roof. operations.
We have collaborated with the cities of Leominster and Fitchburg to create a regional dive team. For the first time In our history,
the Lunenburg Fire Department has a dive team with four certified divers and five shore tenders on the team. The three
communities applied for and received a grant to purchase equipment to help outfit the divers. Considering the number of bodies of
water In Lunenburg, a dive team is an obvious need that offers more protection to the citizens of Lunenburg. By working together,
both citles and our town benefit by having more trained personnel available to all.
Training Is an ongoing requirement of the fire service. in addition to routine training, several of our members completed training in
hazardous. materials rescue. This course prepares rescuers to enter a hazardous environment in fully encapsulated suits In order
to rescue those injured or exposed. This course was developed with the Local Emergency Planning Committee Chairperson. We
are also diligently planning and training for dealing with a pandemic out break.
We continue to provide prompt response to calls for service but also place a high priority on training and increasing our
preparedness for unforeseen events, In order to better serve the residents of our town. We are your first line of defense and we are
proud to serve you.
The Lunenburg Fire Department responded to 1257 emergency calls for assistance and completed 500 Inspections.

POLICE DEPARTMENT
Chief Danlel F. Bourgeols
Another year has passed and I am once again pleased to report that Lunenburg has remained a safe community in which to reside
and visit. Violent crime remains low but alcohol and drug related crime is on the Increase. I must credit our police department
staff for their dedication and perseverance as our community continues to grow and calls for service and administrative dutles
Increase. Our staff of thirteen full-time officers Including myself, fifteen reserve officers and one part-time administrative
commit themselves each day to providing the best professional, responsive public service possible.
This year brought the retirement of Patrolman George Aho. After nearly thirty years of dedicated service to the Town of
Lunenburg, George made the transition Into retirement last July but could not totally cut the ties with our department, as he
remains part of our reserve officer squad working on a part-time basis. I would like to thank George for not only his many years of
dedicated, unselfish service but also his friendship and loyalty to our department. Our best wishes to George and his family.
I would also like to recognize and thank reserve officers Edward Walkonen, Norman Winchester and William Finlay and Michael
Marcil for their respective thirty-five, thirty-four, twelve and twelve years of service to our community. Our best wishes for a safe
and healthy future.
I continue to work with town officials as part of a concerted effort to address our department's staffing and capitol needs in order
to keep pace with rapid town and regional population/commercial growth and the resulting increased demands for services made
on our department. Traffic related issues, motor vehicle accidents, shopliftings, domestic violence calls for service and drug
related issues remain a continuing challenge for our staff. Low staffing levels continue to be issue for our police department as we
are well below the national, state and local surveys pertaining to the ratio of the number of police officers per one thousand
residents. The fact that Lunenburg is geographically located next to Leominster and Fitchburg, the two largest citles in North
Worcester County, plays a significant role in our quest for providing a responsive public service. Crime does not respect town
and city borders. Criminal offenders from surrounding communities have become mobile and have been found operating in our
area. Our officers are working diligently to identify, arrest and prosecute these "regional" offenders and remain pro-active in this
area of crime prevention.
Our department continues to be successful in obtaining Grant Funding to supplement our budget, State and Federally funded
grant monies were obtained to continue Initiatives such as the "Click it or Ticket" and "You Drink, You Drive, You loose"
campalgns in traffic enforcement. These initiatives provide funding for extra patrols to enforce seat belt use, speed enforcement
and drunk driving laws during peak travel times and holidays. A grant from the Governor's Highway Safety Counsel has provided
another fifty bicycle helmets for our school aged children. A State funded Community Policing Grant was obtained to continue our
neighborhood blcycle and walking patrols, boat patrols on Lake Whalom and Lake Shirley as well as safety Initiatives within our
school system and senlor citizen center. We also received additional grant monies to assist In our efforts to combat drug
violations and sales in our area. My thanks to our Police Relief Association who is always there to assist with equipment and
training issues not funded by the Town.

TECHNOLOGY DEPARTMENT
Derek Dufresne, Technology Director
As I read everyone's report, I find It funny that my department Is part of everyone's success, 1 think we lose that perspective on
Ilfe. Athletics, Teachers, Police, Town Hall, everyone we work hard for you. I would like to be even more invisible, that means
that everything Is working and people are productive.
The Technology Department has had a good year. We have been audited twice by firms and have received high marks for
accomplishing as much as we do with much less than most of the Town's and School's In Massachusetts. To those ends, I would
Ilke to open this report with a hearty thank you to the people in my community who have assisted, supported and donated to
Lunenburg's Technology. We are under many constraints and if it weren't for the good spirits that uplift us.on a daily basis, we
would not be S0 successful,
Financially, the Technology Department has been funded and not reduced over the year. Our grants have spiked to an all time
highl We have more money for technology than ever before. However, these grants are targeted in areas and limit the things we
can upgrade. We still need to look at our overall Infrastructure and our compliance with data standards. These Items are
extremely costly. We hope to mitigate these costs to minimize the Impact. Overall, we have progressed and not regressed. This is
a good thing to and for every taxpayer.
However, every year we are asked to do more and more. We need to comply with many standards, acts and laws. The reporting
that we must conduct takes time and cycles away from dedicated staff. Due to HIPAA and CIPA, recent Supreme Court rulings,
and departmental policies, we can't use volunteers for things that we would have readily utilized our resources for. As we add
staff in other locations to support or conduct business, we need to realize that our technological base is this department. We
support everyone and we need to keep more and more things Internal due to legalities. To this end, we have submitted a request
for additional personnel. We have examined possible avenues (volunteers, consultants, firms). Quite simply, we can receive a
greater return on our investment by having an additional worker. We believe this is the best avenue.
in closing, we hope to make further progress for Lunenburg. Email (first Initial and last name @lunenburgonline.com ), website
www.lunenburgonllne.com virtual education, and other projects are the spearhead of our department: We are-proud to serve
every department in Lunenburg and its communityl Thank you for your attention.
ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
The Zoning Board of Appeals heard many petitions during calendar year 2006. There were several application seeking variances,
special permits and dimensional variations. The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the
month on an "as needed" basis. The Zoning Board consists of the following members:- Donald F. Bowen, Chairman, Raymond
Beal, Vice Chairman, Samuel Costa, James Besarkarski, Alfred Gravelle and Associate Members, Hans Wentrup & Peter
Beardmore. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
A Comprehensive Permit was filed by Hollis Hills, LLC on February 13, 2006. The project, which is proposed on Hollis Road and
West Street, Includes plans for the construction of 146 townhouse condominium units on 33.74 acres of land. This application is
still pending before the Board.
The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as amended,
from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from the
Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative
Official.
4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B. The
Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under Chapter 40B by
developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 during regular business hours.
Zoning Board applications are available in the office of the Town Clerk.

MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL VOCATIONAL TECHNICAL SCHOOL (MONTY TECH)
James R. Culkeen, Superintendent-Director
The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District Committee presents this report to the citizens of the District. The
Committee consists of twenty-two (22) members - four from Fitchburg, represented by LeRoy Clark, Mark Louney, Robert
Campbell and Brian Walker, two from Gardner, represented by Helen Lepkowski and Alice Anderson and one from each of the
communities of Ashburnham - represented by Diane Swenson, Ashby - represented by Warren Landry, Athol - represented by
Toni L. Phillips, Barre - represented by John Scott, Harvard - Vacancy, Holden - represented by Nancy Anderson, Hubbardston -
represented by Jacqueline B. Kaminski, Lunenburg - represented by Barbara Reynolds, Petersham - represented by Edward
Simms, Phillipston - represented by Eric Olson, Princeton - represented by John Mollica, Royalston - represented by Mary C.
Barclay, Sterling - represented by Dr. Kenneth I.H. Williams, Templeton - represented by Henry Richard, Westminster -
represented by Jeffrey Schutt and Winchendon - represented by Burton E. Gould, Jr.
At the organizational meeting held In June, Mr. Mark Louney of Fitchburg was elected Chairperson, Jeffrey Schutt of Westminster
was elected Vice Chair, Norman LeBlanc was elected Treasurer, and James R. Culkeen, Superintendent-Director, was elected
District Clerk.
FINANCIAL REPORT
The FY 2007 budget was prepared with considerable effort from the staff, administration and School Committee. Challenges faced
during this budget development process continue to Include the costs of energy, health care, infrastructure improvements and
transportation.
Our efforts to curtail costs in these areas have been moderately successful. Recent initiatives including our Energy Management
System and Health Care Advisory Committee have attempted to control these costs.
The final Educational Plan totaled $19,488,723 for Fiscal Year 2007 and was unanimously approved by the cities and towns. The
District was audited in August of 2006 as part of our yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson & Heath from
Greenfield, Massachusetts and an "Excellent" report Is being prepared.
STUDENT BODY
Monty Tech's October 1, 2006 enrollment included 1,301 students in grades nine through twelve. Students are represented from
every community in the district. Ashburnham (49), Ashby (30), Athol (103), Barre (28), Fitchburg (391), Gardner (137), Harvard (4),
Holden (44) Hubbardston (40), Lunenburg (62), Petersham (3), Phillipston (15), Princeton (16), Royalston (26), Sterling (58),
Templeton (67), Westminster (66), Winchendon (144).
The Guidance Department/Admissions Department received 728 applications for admission in September 2006. Of these, 641 were
for the available ninth grade openings. Due to the great retention rate of our upper classes, we were only able to admit about 15
students to the tenth and eleventh grades.
Monty Tech continued to offer varied opportunities for students, parents, and community members to learn about and visit the
school. In October, over seventeen hundred district eighth graders participated in Tour Days. Students toured many of our twenty
vocational-technical areas and learned about the challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular
programs. This Open House, as well as last spring's Open House, was attended by hundreds of students, parents, and community
members from throughout the district.
The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) continued to attract large numbers of seventh and eighth graders during both five-week
sessions during school year 2005-2006. Participants had opportunities to attend hands-on after school sessions In a varlety of
vocational/technical areas, A satisfaction survey rated this program 4.7 out of 5. The fall 2006 enrollment was the highest ever at
310 seventh and eighth graders.
In June 2006, the class of 2006 graduated 261 seniors. These graduates received diplomas and Technical Certificates,
Approximately, 95% of the graduates planned to continue their education at the post-secondary level, or employment, while
another 5% planned to enter military service.
The Scholarship Committee awarded approximately $20,000 in scholarships to members of the Class of 2006. In addition, they
awarded $9,000 to the Practical Nursing graduates. Once again, local (Business Education Enrichment Fund) and state
organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates In the form
of generous donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves are grateful for this support.
26
===PAGE 30===
Our dropout rate continued to decline with an Impressive rate of 1% for school year 05/06.
The Tech Prep Articulation Agreements with local colleges continued to play an important role in helping our graduates enter
college through credits earned at Monty Tech. Half of our graduates entered a two or four year college. This number has grown
over the years.
ACHIEVEMENT
Based on its tenth grade MCAS scores, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to see an improvement
during the 2005-2006 school year in student achievement in both English/Language Arts and Mathematics. In the spring of 2006,
Monty Tech's passing rate on the mathematics portion of the test climbed from 89% to 91%, while the percentage of students
scoring in the proficient and advanced categories in math jumped from 49% to 59%. The school's passing rate on the English
portion of the test also climbed from 94% to 96%, while the students scoring In the proficient and advanced categories in English
went from 49% to 52%. In math, Monty Tech's MCAS scores rank In the top 20% of voc-tech-schools statewide, and in English, Its
MCAS scores rank in the top 50% of voc-tech schools statewide.

FACULTY AND STAFF
In the 2005-2006 school year, the School Committee welcomed new faculty, specialists and aldes. The staff included 56 vocational
teachers, 3 vocational specialists, 2 vocational teaching assistants, 45 academic teachers, 2 MCAS specialists, 4 guidance

SKILLS USA
Monty Tech students continued to excel in the national Skills USA Program. There were 144 medalists when our local
competitions were over. Students won 44 medals at the district conference in Tewksbury, and 34 State conference winners shared
their medals with the school in April 2006. We took a proud 2ⁿᵈ place at the State Conference in Community Service. Of the 34 State
Conference winners, 10 of them competed at the National Skills USA Conference In Kansas City, Missouri in June 2006. We also
had one student attend the National Conference representing Massachusetts as a State Officer. The National Competitors
represented not only Monty Tech, but the State of Massachusetts as well. All competitors placed in the top 5% in the country, and
the Team Works Team took home the bronze medal for the two-day competition in which they participated.
FIRST ROBOTICS
Monty Tech's Robotics Team was one of six teams that were finalists in the 2006 Granite State Regional Robotics Competition.
Their alliance received the 2ⁿᵈ place award. The Granite State Regional FIRST (For Inspiration and Recognition of Science and
Technology) Robotics Competition was held at the Verizon Wireless Arena, March 2-4, 2006. This year's challenge - "Alm High" -
had teams competing in three versus three-team alliances within a four-round match. The arena included three goals for each
alliance, two corner goals and one center goal. The competition required launching balls and pushing balls into goals. Three
human players participated in each alliance, scoring additional points by throwing retrieved balls over a Plexiglas shield. The
team was also selected as the winner of the Motorola Quality Award, celebrating machine robustness in concept and fabrication.
WOMEN IN TECHNOLOGY
During the 2005-2006 school year, three seniors and three sophomores participated In the Women in Technology Program in
partnership with Tyco Simplex/Grinnell. Every other Tuesday, the young women, accompanied by Mrs. Angela Ikonen and Mrs.
Helen Simms, traveled to the Westminster site to participate in the program. Other schools Involved Included Oakmont Regional,
Gardner High School, Narragansett Regional and Leominster Center for Technical Education. The project for the year was to
develop computer software that would assist the company and its customers in maintaining records on leased materials.

SPECIAL SERVICES
During the 2005-2006 school year, the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided special education
services to over two hundred students. In September 2006, the Support Services Department Is working with approximately 197
students.
The revised law, Individuals with Disabilities Education Act (IDEA-04), took effect July 1, 2005 with official and final part B
regulations being completed on 9/16/06. It provides for more services to students transitioning from Monty Tech to careers,
college, or further training. Since the intent of this law is to educate as many students as possible in the general education
classrooms, the staff at Monty Tech has embarked on a major shift in how we teach and support students with disabilities. The
major focus of special education services will be Inclusive education using universal design to ensure that every student with a
disability is educated In the "least restrictive environment."
The student services at Monty Tech are available to all students. We have a full-time school nurse with a part-time assistant who
administers medications, performs state mandated health screening tests, and provides, when necessary, health information to
the student's IEP team meeting. We have a full-time school social worker who is able to participate on teams and assist students
who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. Our
students have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor and a part-time school psychologist. Both of these
people are available for scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency treatment or crisis intervention. We recently
added a full-time psychologist to complete all testing and evaluations for Initial and three year re-evaluations. A full-time speech
pathologist is available to service students with disabilities, assess students, and consult with all teachers in the building.
The Special Education Program at Monty Tech is reviewed every May in accordance with regulatory requirements. The results of
this evaluation are used to improve the special education procedures and programs at Monty Tech. The Parent Advisory Council
participates in this review and evaluation.
The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District adheres to federal and state law regarding confidentiality of
personally Identifiable student Information.
