TECHNOLOGY
Monty Tech continues to provide technology resources, which exceed both state averages and benchmarks. Our Inventory
Includes workstations using both Windows and Macintosh operating systems. The budget generally allows us to keep the age of
our workstations at 5 years or less, which Is somewhat less than Ideal. However, placement of systems based on applications
requirements allows us to continue to productively utilize older systems.
Overall network performance, especially Internet traffic, has been an ongoing concern. Last year, we tripled the available Internet
bandwidth resulting in a significant Improvement with regard to internet access. In a typical day at Monty Tech, over 550,000 web
pages are accessed by our students and staff. As reported last year, upgrades to network infrastructure, particularly old category
3 wiring remains a priority. The Installation of managed switches has allowed us to control traffic, which might otherwise have
problems on the older parts of the network.
We reported last year that we were decreasing the emphasis on laptops. This issue was revisited by the administration, and we
added significantly to our wireless laptop Inventory. Wireless access points in the school continue to expand. We have also
added a terminal based Windows Computer Lab using the Citrix System. All software resides and processing occurs on a single
server in the Technology Office, reducing the time required to Install software, upgrades and perform other maintenance tasks.
Along with increasing the Internet bandwidth, we also installed a new firewall, which effectively controls content filtering, virus and
hacking attacks. A new e-mail system has provided marked Improvement with regard to controlling spam.
Probably, the most significant challenge to the Technology Office revolves around insuring that network performance remains at
acceptable levels. The numerous servers in place, as well as network traffic problems, requires a considerable allocation of
resources on a daily basis.
GRANTS AND CONTRACTS
Monty Tech pursues grant funding on a continual basis. The funding procured assists the school in providing an array of
educational and social services to the students. For fiscal year 2006, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
$882,169. Programs funded by these grants include Improving Teacher Quality, Enhancing Education Through Technology,
Special Education allocation and Special Education Program Improvement, Title V, Title I, Occupational Education - Vocational
Skills, State Leadership - Research, State Leadership Curriculum Development, Summer Academic Support and Perkins
Certificate of Occupational Proficiency.
CONCLUDING STATEMENT
In conclusion, the School Committee, administration, students and staff extend their thanks to the cities and towns in the district
for their continued support of Monty Tech and vocational-technical education.
We are committed to providing the best educational opportunities for workforce development In the district; and again, we will
assist the cities and towns In the Monty Tech District in any way possible. Thank you for your support.

LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Our Vision: The Lunenburg Public Schools prides itself on excellence. We provide a child-centered environment that challenges
all learners to achieve their highest potential. This is accomplished through a partnership with a supportive community.
Our Mission: We prepare our students for life-long learning and responsible community membership.
Our Guiding Beliefs:
Education is a community partnership.
Learning is reflective, creative, on-going and takes place in all environments.
A successful school actively engages students and staff.
All students have talents and can excel.
Professional growth is fundamental to Instructional excellence.
All students are capable of learning and are entitled to instructional excellence.
Staff and students are entitled to a safe, healthy and positive learning environment.
An atmosphere of mutual respect is essential for learning.

SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent
The Lunenburg Public Schools prides Itself on being a professional learning community dedicated to the learning of each student.
Throughout our schools this past year we focused on improving student performance, increased academic rigor, and the
development of our students as citizens and responsible community members. In the reports that follow, the building principals
will highlight the accomplishments of the students, faculty, and staff. We have developed common assessments at all levels and
sought to improve our communication with parents about student performance by revising our reports cards at the primary and
elementary schools. The students and faculty of Turkey Hill Middle School received statewide recognition based upon their
MCAS scores, and 30 percent of the Class of 2006 received the Adams Scholarship. Lunenburg students in the aggregate, as well
as all subgroups, met performance targets required to achieve adequate yearly progress. As a result of the dedication of a small
group of dedicated citizens as well as the support of the community as a whole, the track and field continues to become a reality:
We are looking forward to hosting track and field competitions in Lunenburg for the first time in over a decade. Finally, our
schools have written and Implemented new curriculum for Instructional technology, and we have a new social-emotional
curriculum that focuses on the development of responsible, engaged citizens and community members.
This past year our schools sald farewell to twelve retirees with over 302 years of service to the schools and community. Those
retired include: James Besarkarski 38 years; Carolyn Brochu, 37 years; Marylynn Conrad, 37 years; Paul Romano, 35 years,
Roger St Jean; 34 years; Ellen Gillham, 32 years; Mark O'Malley, 30 years; Rita Maistrellis, 20 years; Jacqueline Stanton; 16 years;
Joel Underwood 12 years; and Judith Wood, 11 years. We want to express our appreciation to these dedicated and committed
professionals and extend our best wishes to them In their retirement.
Our facilities represent the biggest challenge to our Instructional program. We lack the infrastructure to support the technology
necessary for today's educational programs. As we approach our next high school accreditation, we must address facility
inadequacies. We took the first step in addressing these concerns by undertaking a school district facilities study which was
approved at Town Meeting last year. This year the School Committee held a series of public forums designed to hear the
community's ideas about our school facilities. The study Included evaluation of all existing school facilities on our central
campus. Earlier this year the Massachusetts School Building Authority (MSBA) also did a study of the physical conditions of all
school buildings in the state. Both these studies concluded that Lunenburg High School is the school most in need of attention
due to structural, programmatic, and enrollment Inadequacies and that we should consider submitting a Statement of Interest to
MSBA outlining these deficiencies. The MSBA may then invite the town to submit an application based on the information
provided In the Statement of Interest. The facilities study also included demographic and enrollment projections and concluded
that with new development, over the next ten years we can anticipate an increase of approximately 150 more resident students in
grades K-12. Enrollment for the past four years is reported below.
Throughout the year our students have distinguished themselves and this community at art competitions, musical performances,
foreign language competitions, math competitions, on the athletic playing field and at a wide variety of student leadership
activities. Our students are active In a wide variety of service activities, volunteering many hours to support the community. Each
year I receive calls from people In and outside of Lunenburg complimenting our students on their contributions, attitudes, and
behavior. We are proud of the manner in which our students represent and serve our community.
The Lunenburg Public Schools is a vibrant learning community that focuses on student achievement, Involves parents, and
engages its faculty and staff. We encourage you to visit our website (www.lunenburgonline.com) to keep up-to-date on the
numerous school activities and initlatives. Thank you for your support and Involvement and for the opportunity to serve the multi-
talented, hard working and caring students of Lunenburg.

HIGH SCHOOL
Michael Barney, Principal
Lunenburg High School was a hub for academic and social activity in 2006-2007. This summer, while many students were basking
on the beach, seven Lunenburg High School students were taking part in the Presidential Academy at Mount Wachusett
Community College. The Presidential Academy was sponsored by MWCC to promote and support academic excellence and
student achievement to local school districts. Lunenburg had the greatest number of student participants in the academy, which
consisted of an 8 week "brick and click" Macroeconomics course combining online learning using internet technology with class
meetings on the college's Gardner campus. The students earned three college credits and high school credit for their
participation in the course.
SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
The STEP (Senior Transition Experience Program) students held a welcome breakfast for their sponsors in September in the-high
school library. Beginning its 4th year, the STEP program engages community mentors with high school seniors to expand learning
beyond the classroom, and give students a personalized experience tallored to their post-secondary Interests. The breakfast was
a chance for LHS to recognize and thank our mentors, have the students introduce the mentors to each other and to celebrate this
Important part of senior year as recognized by several national studies, including Breaking Ranks II.
As a result of tremendous community support, the LHS Marching Band debuted new unlforms this year. The blue and sliver
uniforms shined under the lights of the last football game, and made our Blue Knights Marching Band look as awesome as they
sound. We would like to thank the Individual and community sponsors, as well as the dedicated group of parents who
spearheaded this monumental effort to make this dream a reality.
MCAS RESULTS
We were pleased to announce the 2006 grade 10 MCAS results. Results released indicated 79% of our students scored
advanced/proficient In Mathematics, Including a 10% rise In the advanced category and 80% scored advanced/proficient in English
Language Arts. Our scores exceeded statewide averages, which were 67% of students scoring advanced/proficient In
Mathematics and 69% scoring advanced/proficient in English Language Arts.
We were also very pleased to learn that 41 seniors at Lunenburg High School received the state's John and Abigail Adams
Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent of their
district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the
English and Math exams.
HOMECOMING AND ATHLETICS
Our annual homecoming tradition was again a wonderful town-wide event, with the parade down Main Street showcasing floats
built by all four classes followed by the football game and the dance. Our fall athletic seasons were very successful, highlighted
by the girl's cross country team making history as the first LHS girls' squad qualifying for the state championship meet, and our
field hockey team going deep into district play. In the winter, our girl's basketball team had won the league at press time and
entered the district tournament with high hopes to bring a championship back to LHS.
The Renaissance Rally at Thanksgiving prepared our student body for the annual game VS. St. Bernard's as well as recognizing the
300 students who achieved honor roll status for first term.
COMMUNITY BUILDING
In January, the entire student body was treated to a piano performance by recording artist and planist Brittany Maler. Brittany Is a
17 year old young woman who is blind and autistic. She also has a remarkable talent as a planist, and travels the United States
performing for audiences, spreading the message that a disability doesn't have to keep you from excelling in something that you
love to do. Brittany currently performs over 15,000 songs from memory, and adds about 2000 songs per year to her repertoire,
practicing up to 12 hours per day, Her inspiring performance was first seen by Jenna Jacobs, Best Buddies President, at the
National Best Buddies Conference this summer, where Jenna was S0 moved that she came home determined to convince the LHS
chapter to raise the funds to bring Brittany to perform in Lunenburg. The chapter, through fund raising efforts, raised over $1400
to sponsor both the performance for the LHS concert and an evening performance open to the public. Both shows proved to be
moving experiences for the audiences, as well as enjoyable sing-alongs, including requests from "Plano Man" and "Bohemian
Rhapsody" to "Rent". What was most moving was the acceptance of our students for Brittany as just another teenager with a
particular talent.

CURRICULUM
New additions to our curriculum this year Included courses in Global Issues and Digital Communications, as well as expansion of
the College and Career Portfolio program for Juniors. An Advisory program for the freshmen, following recommendations to
personalize learning and assist with the transition from grade 8 to grade 9, was also Implemented as a goal of our school
Improvement plan. Our music and art programs continue to thrive, producing award winners in the Boston Globe Scholastic Art
show and the Central District and State Chorus, as well as fantastic performances at our concerts and displays all year round.

TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL
Last year Turkey HIII Middle School was the only middle school in the entire Commonwealth to win the prestigious Compass
School Award and, in fact, the only middle school to win the award in four years. This year the Department of Education bestowed
still another honor on Turkey HIII; THMS won the John Monbouquette School Improvement Leadership Award. The DOE surveyed
all the past winners of the Compass Award, of which there are many, to determine which schools have continued to make
progress above what is expected on MCAS tests. Out of all of these schools only thirteen schools scored above expectations In
both English/language arts and mathematics. Turkey Hill Middle School was one of the thirteen! However, when the DOE focused
just on mathematics, Turkey Hill Middle was the very top scoring compass school in the entire commonwealth. What does this
mean? When the DOE looked at the best performing schools based on Improvement In MCAS results in both ELA and
mathematics THMS was one of the very best, and when they looked at just mathematics we were the best of the bestl What a
terrific honor for the students and teachers of Turkey Hill. What a terrific honor for all K-8 teachers who share this award. What a
terrific honor for the parents of all the students and the Town of Lunenburg.
In addition to the above awards THMS distinguished Itself In other ways. The Just4Kids website compares results on MCAS
scores from comparable kinds of schools. This year Lunenburg was placed with schools like Carlisle, Westford, Lexington,
Lincoln, Sharon, Wellesley, Weston, etc. all of whom spend a great deal more as judged by per pupil spending than does
Lunenburg. Yet Turkey HIII Middle continues year after year to perform as well as these schools. In fact our results are as follows:
GradeSubjectRanking
6thMathematics 14th out of a pool of 244 schools
6thELA 69 out of 244
7thELA 25 out of 260
7thMathematics 54 out of 304
8thELA 61 out of 319
8thMathematics 118 out of 319
8thSclence 66 out of 319
We are quite proud of these results. All of these awards and results clearly demonstrate the quality of our teachers and our
students. Considering that our per pupil expenditures are well below the median cost in the Commonwealth, the efforts made by
the entire Lunenburg educational community is commendable.
The school athletics program continued in its second year at Turkey HIII. Once again our students competed in cross country,
field hockey, football, cheerleading and boys/girls basketball. It is a terrific experience for our students to represent themselves
and their school in athletic contests. The lessons learned from hard work, team work, as well as the camaraderle add a great deal
to the personal side of attending Turkey HIII. Student Government, Yearbook, and skilng continue to round out the opportunities
students have at THMS.
Another significant accomplishment for THMS was the increase in the number of students taking algebra in eighth grade.
Previously about 33% of our eighth graders took algebra; this year we have raised that number to 66%. Previously we were
slightly below the state average; now we are well above it. This change represents the constantly Improving mathematics program
In the earller grades. Students need to come to 8th grade with an excellent math background to take algebra. More and more
students are doing just that as seen in the increased percentage; It reflects the rigor of the math program.
THMS spent a great deal of time providing time for our staff to develop common assessments and Understanding by Design units
of Instruction. Both of these Insure curriculum consistency across the grade levels. When all students in a grade are taught the
same units of instruction or use the same science lab techniques, or have the same writing experiences, all students Improve and
all students are challenged.
Another highlight of the year was a visit by Terri Francona, the manager of the Boston Red Sox. A student at THMS won a national
contest sponsored by Raytheon and because of that Raytheon chose our school as one of the two schools that Coach Francona
visited. This was another terrific honor and a wonderful day for our students.
It was a grand and glorious year for Turkey Hill Middle School. As always we want to thank the citizens of Lunenburg for
supporting us and giving us the opportunity to enjoy success.
Thank you, Lunenburg.

LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
Cynthia Daukantas, Principal
The Lunenburg Primary School continues to strive toward educating the total child. We utilize a variety of Instructional methods
and modalities in our approach to meet the Individual needs of every child. We believe that the success of our programs is
dependent upon a healthy and productive partnership between home and school. Toward that end, parents must play an active
role In many of the dally programs and routines of their child's educational program.
With an enrollment of four hundred twenty-four students, class sizes range from eighteen to twenty-four. Because research
indicates that class sizes of twenty and under in the early years provide lasting educational benefits to young children, we strive to
keep our kindergarten through grade 2 classes smaller.
During the summer of 2006 summer school funding was limited. Through fundraising efforts, which Included two book fairs,
School Linked Services was able to provide the funding for kindergarten and first grade children to receive Instruction for the
month of July. Mrs. Lisa Stone, kindergarten teacher, and Mrs. Barbara Nelson, grade 1 teacher, were excited about the progress
that all twenty children made. This progress was further noted in our grade one and two fall reading testing. I am hopeful that
through our continuing fundraising efforts, we will-be able to offer support to both kindergarten and grade one children during the
summer of '07.
Through the support of parents and to meet the needs of parents and children, two full day tuition based kindergarten programs
were created again this fall. We are excited about offering a second full day program to kindergarteners, and look forward to
when we can offer this program to all five year olds. We are extremely pleased and proud that our efforts in securing grant funds
to begin the process of planning for full day kindergarten for all students has resulted in a State Transition Planning Grant of
$30,000. This money will allow us to begin the process of a self-study through NAEYC In anticipation of national accreditation, as
well as to begin purchasing some much needed materials/equipment. We are hopeful that Lunenburg will be able to secure some
portion of full day kindergarten implementation funding for next fall.
The Primary School continues to strive toward academic excellence. Reading testing was completed this fall and again In January
on every child in kindergarten, grade 1, and grade 2. The mid-winter testing shows significant Improvements In all areas of
reading (phonemic segmentation, fluency, letter Identification, sound symbol). We are hopeful that our end of year testing will
validate that all children are reading on grade level. According to our School Improvement Plan, it is our goal that 80% of our
students at each grade level will read on grade level In June.
This year the faculty has worked to create quarterly assessments in math to monitor children's progress. Each administration of
this assessment includes an open response problem. Teachers have met to discuss their Individual classroom results as well as
to plan the needed Interventions for those children not meeting the standard. Our School Improvement Plan identifies the goal
to be 80% of students at each grade level will attain 80% of the standards in math.
The Primary School Improvement Plan focuses upon reading, writing, and math, as well as revising / updating our report cards and
writing exemplars. As the curriculum changes, our report cards needs to reflect the current standards so that we can
communicate student progress to parents. At this point in the year, report cards have been updated at all grade levels and will be
submitted to the School Committee for their approval In May as part of our School Improvement Plan update.
The Primary School Council meets the first Thursday of each month to discuss school Issues and to develop its annual School
Improvement Plan. Parents and community members are welcome to attend. Members of the Council for the 2006 . 2007 school
year are:
Principal:
Cyndy Daukantas
Teachers:
Shella Bilotta, Nancy Chapdelaine, and Lisa Stone
Parents:
Carla Cappucci, Karin Menard, Jason Rowley, and Heather Swift
Community Representative
Pamela Penna
School Committee Rep:
Michael Clark
In closing the faculty and staff of the Primary School is extremely proud of the work they do with young children. We are deeply
committed to our own professional growth and to student learning. Only through working with parents are we able to accomplish
the goals set forth by the State and district.

SPECIAL SERVICES DEPARTMENT
Elaine E. Blaisdell, Student Services Coordinator
We continue to focus on our mission of continuous Improvement for all students. Administrators, educators, and parents
collaboratively work to develop Individualized Educational Plans for students that meet federal and state guidelines for least
restrictive setting and access to the curriculum.
There are two hundred and seventy-four (274) students with disabilities receiving special education or related service In
Lunenburg. This represents 14.8 % of our total student population, This percentage is above the national average of 12%
and less than the state average of 16.3 %. Of the total number of students receiving services, ten students received services
outside the district during the 2006/2007 school year. Of the ten students, eight are In day placements and two are In
residential placements. The breakdown of disability categories is as follows; Autism- 7%, Communication Disability-25%,
Developmental Disability-16%, Emotional Disability- 3%, Health Disability- 13.5%, Intellectual Disability- 6.5%, Multiple
Disabilities- 2.5%, Neurological Disability- 2.5%, Physical Disability- 1%, Sensory Visual Impairment- >1%, Specific Learning
Disability- 21%. Fifty-one (51) students Identified with a disability receive accommodations via a 504 plan in Lunenburg.
Special Needs students reached the expected progress level to achieve adequate yearly progress again this year. This is
due to the efforts of our students in concert with our education staff and parents having high expectations, rigorous specific
instruction and consistent progress monitoring of skills for students. The staff continues to use the triple threat: results
teams, understanding by design for lesson planning and GEMS curriculum to accomplish our goals of continuous
Improvement for students. At Individual team meetings parents and teachers engage In active discussions about the data
and research base practices necessary to address individual student's strengths and weaknesses. They continue to think
outside the box in developing plans that meet the unique needs of each student in the least restrictive environment.
Instructional teaming efforts continue to expand and teams continue to refine their skills In developing accommodations
and lessons that help students reach their educational goals. The district has committed resources and time In the areas of
common planning time and professional development to build effective Instructional teams across the district.
The Parent Advisory Counsel of Special Achievers in Lunenburg (PACSAL) has been an active support group for parents
and administration. Currently we have 15 active members. This year we have been joined by parents of students whose
children receive Title I services. Meetings are scheduled for the last Tuesday of each month, 7:00PM, at the Lunenburg
Public Library, The executive board is made up of Co-Chairs: Deborah Carter, Kelly Harvey, and Treasurer: Kelly Moriera,
and Secretary: Sherry Bishop. Presentation Topics this year have covered ADD and ADHD by Dr. Karen Bresnahan; Social
Skills and Behavioral Interventions by Dr. Russ Maguire and Melissa Maguire, The Language of Learning by Karen Martin;
MCAS Workshop by Karen Martin and Elaine Blaisdell; Right and Responsibility in Special Education by Sandra Osborne
and Elaine Blaisdell , to name a few. The PACSAL has completed several fundraising activities to support speakers on
various special education related topics. They have provided Information on disabilities and their organization at various
school and town functions.
In summary, The Lunenburg Public Schools take great pride in our ability to provide a variety of special needs services to
meet the varying needs of our students. We have an experienced hard working staff that works together with parents to
provide these necessary services. Our parents continue to be equal partners in our efforts to develop challenging and
focused educational programs for their child. We remain committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools mission "We prepare
our students for life long learning and responsible community membership." This is a total community effort and we
encourage ALL Lunenburg residents to Join with us to create positive experiences and futures for our children.
For additional information, please contact Elaine E. Blaisdell, Student Services Coordinator, Special Services Office, 1033
Massachusetts Ave, (978) 582-4114.

===PAGE 44===
INSTRUCTIONAL SERVICES DEPARTMENT
Karen Martin, Director
During this past year, the Instructional Services Office continued to focus on three areas critical to the District's mission: (a)
curriculum and assessment development, (b) continuous Improvement for ALL and (c) meeting the academic, social and
emotional needs of our students. Following is a summary of activities and results in each of these areas.
CURRICULUM AND ASSESSMENT
This spring the school committee formally approved the new Instructional Technology curriculum for the district. This
curriculum documents the benchmarks, best practices and curriculum integration using Instructional technology. This
summer the school committee approved the newly developed GEMS curriculum. Academic, social and emotional learning
now has a formal curriculum PreK-12 with school-wide expectations. Throughout the school year, each building has been
Involved in developing common assessments, Understanding by Design units and curriculum maps. These tools will
ensure students have common learning experiences and that there is regular monitoring of student performance against a
standard. Teachers are using the assessment results to Improve student performance.
Our district uses our MCAS (Massachusetts Comprehensive Assessment System) results and our Adequate Yearly Progress
results to monitor overall performance and to provide specific feedback for teachers, students and our program.
Our analysis this year was complicated by new tests, changes in testing format and comparing different groups of students
from year to year. We can draw some conclusions that will help us move forward and Improve Instruction for all students.
Key findings:
The district's overall performance on the 2006 MCAS mirrors the state performance. Our challenge is to move
beyond the state comparison to comparlsons with the proficient and advanced levels.
The district over time has a positive trend line In all subjects. Our challenge is to accelerate improved student
performance In all subjects.
Meeting AYP for all of our subgroups, particularly with our students with disabilities, continues to be an area of
focus for the school district. Our challenge Is to develop Instructional strategies and models of Interventions for
students at risk and students with disabilities that significantly improve student performance.
The district has been successful In moving students Into the proficiency levels. Our next challenge is moving more
students into the advanced level.
Open response (writing) Items are at the state level, while our multiple choice items are at higher levels. Our next
challenge is to Increase the quality of written response.
CONTINUOUS IMPROVEMENT FOR ALL
The No Child Left Behind legislation provides an opportunity to carefully examine performance results for all students.
Every district in the nation received Annual Yearly Progress results for this mid-cycle. The Lunenburg Public Schools met
AYP goals in the aggregate (all students) and for students in all subgroups. This data helps guide the school councils,
administrative team and Results teams to plan for appropriate interventions, programming and professional development.
School structures and collaboration focused on student learning ensure all students Improve. The Lunenburg Public
Schools has Results Teams, Understanding by Design training, a comprehensive professional development program and
new teacher Induction program to support student learning.
Results Teams meet one time per month during faculty meeting time. The teams use data to set goals, plan teaching
strategies and assess the results. This process ensures a regular review of student performance and effective teaching
strategies.
This year, grade level teams are developing common UBD assessments and grade/subject assessments in all content areas.
Through ongoing analysis of student results, refinement of units and Increased awareness of effective assessments and
instructional practices, student performance will improve. The UBD units provide additional measures in non-MCAS
subjects and provide additional curriculum materials for new teachers. Ongoing and sustained professional development
continues in a range of areas. This year, graduate level courses were offered In New Teacher Induction, Responsive
Teaching, Curriculum Implementation and Junior Great Books.
Our new teacher induction program provides ongoing support for new teachers to the district. New teachers participate in a
two-day orientation, regular meetings during the school year and professional development for new teachers.
41
===PAGE 45===
GEMS- ACADEMIC, SOCIAL AND EMOTIONAL LEARNING
This year is the first year of Implementation of the district-wide GEMs curriculum. The vision of GEMs is ensuring all
students grow to their potential, develop empathy towards others, value the importance of self-discipline and become
contributing members of the community. This year, all schools are working to establish school-wide routines and
structures that support academic, social and emotional learning for ALL children. Teachers have been Involved In
professional development, Responsive classroom training and are working on UBD units that support the GEMs curriculum.
SUMMARY
The Instructional Services Office has a strong partnership with the Individual schools, the administrative team, faculty and
community. The combined and integrated efforts of all parties ensure continued focus on Improving student performance
and strive to provide teachers, administrators, students and the community with the necessary data, educational research,
professional development, support and resources needed to Improve student performance.
For additional Information on any of these topics, please contact Karen Martin, Instructional Services Director, at 978-582-
4118 or e-mail kmartin@lunenburgonllne.com or see the Instructional Services Website under administrative departments at
the district school website.
SCHOOL FACILITIES
John Londa, Director of Facilities & Grounds
The focus of the school maintenance effort for FY07 heavily stressed energy conservation measures with changes to the
control of building exterior lighting and replacement of selected Interior lighting. The school department and selected town
facilities entered into contracts with third party suppliers for electricity and natural gas in order to reduce cost and stabilize
pricing for energy. The Initial estimate is that electricity use will have been reduced by in excess of ten percent. A second
focus of the maintenance effort continues to be improvements to compliance with the American Disabilities Act (ADA),
Kessler McGinness and Associates completed a complete review of accessibility to Lunenburg Facilities, which has
provided a direction for continued efforts.
The architectural firm of Drummey Rosane Anderson, Inc. was. selected to complete a long-range facilities plan for the
School Department that studies the Impact of town growth and conditions of school facilities. The additional focus of the
study was a feasibility study of options for planning for the future of the 50-year-old Lunenburg High School. While the
study has not been finalized as of this report DRA Is recommending replacement of the existing high school based upon the
extensive and Intrusive work required to modernize Lunenburg High School. The school committee is expected to receive
the final report in March 2007.
Capital Improvements for FY07 were delayed due to the failure to fund the capital article in the annual town election. In Dec
2006 at a special town meeting funding was approved for an upgrade of security measures at the 55-year-old Passios
Elementary School. In the spring of 2007, a new door audio/video camera system will be Installed on two of the entryways
into the school, a new alarm system, and card access for the staff and replacement of all exterior doors.
The Lunenburg Track and Field Athletic Association continued work on a soccer and track facility between the High School
and the Middle School. The installation of running surface on the track was completed in September and the facility opened
to the public. Town funding for the project will speed the completion of the project In the remainder of 2007.

FOLLOWING ARE THOSE RECORDS OF BIRTHS, MARRIAGES, AND DEATHS THAT EITHER OCCURRED IN OUR TOWN OR RECORDS
WHICH WERE FORWARDED TO THIS TOWN FROM OTHER MUNICIPALITIES IN THE STATE. LUNENBURG RESIDENTS ARE URGED TO
NOTIFY THE TOWN CLERK IF IN-STATE RECORDS HAVE NOT BEEN RECEIVED AND RECORDED. IF A BIRTH, MARRIAGE, OR DEATH
OCCURRED OUTSIDE THE STATE, THE SAME MAY BE RECORDED IN THE LUNENBURG TOWN CLERK'S OFFICE IF CERTIFIED
RECORDS ARE PRESENTED. IN SOME CASES THE TOWN CLERK'S RECORDS ARE SUPPLEMENTED BY RECORDS OF BURIALS
WHICH APPEAR ELSEWHERE IN THIS REPORT. IN ORDER FOR THESE RECORDS TO ALSO BE INCLUDED IN THE OFFICIAL VITAL
STATISTICS RECORDS, THE FAMILY OF THE DECEASED SHOULD PRESENT A CERTIFIED COPY OF THE DEATH CERTIFICATE TO THE
TOWN CLERK. IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE GENERAL LAWS, MARRIAGES WHICH OCCUR HERE BUT RELATE TO LICENSES WHICH
WERE OBTAINED ELSEWHERE IN THE STATE ARE NO LONGER INCLUDED IN OUR OFFICIAL TOWN RECORDS.

Question 7
Shall the Town of Lunenburg accept sections 3 to 7 Inclusive, of MCL Chapter 44B, as approved by the legislative body,
a summary of which appears below?
PREC A
PREC B
PREC C
PREC D
TOTAL
YES
202

810
NO
258
224
258
253
993
BLANKS
45
31
37
28
141
TOTAL
505.
417
491
531
1944
Sections 3 to 7 of Chapter 44B of the General Laws of Massachusetts, also known as the Community Preservation Act,
established a dedicated funding source to (1) acquire open space which includes land for park and recreational uses
and the protection of public drinking water well fields, aquifers and recharge areas, wetlands, farm land, forests,
marshes, beaches, scenic areas, wildlife preserves and other conservation areas; (2) acquire and restore historic
buildings and sites; and (3) create affordable housing.
In the Town of Lunenburg, the funding for these community preservation purposes will be a surcharge of 1% on the
annual property tax assessed on real property and annual distributions made by the state from a trust fund created by
the Act. If approved, the following will be exempt from the surcharge; 1) $100,000.00 of the value of each taxable parcel
of residential real property. A taxpayer receiving an exemption of real property authorized by Chapter 59 of the General
Laws of Massachusetts or any other law is exempt from any surcharge on real property established under the act. A
taxpayer receiving a regular property tax abatement will also receive a pro rata reduction in surcharge.
A Community Preservation Committee will be established by by-law to study community preservation resources,
possibilities and needs and to make annual recommendations to Town Meeting on spending the funds. At least ten
(10%) percent of the funds for each fiscal year will be spent or reserved for later spending on each of the Act's three
community preservation purposes; (1) open space (excluding land for recreational use); (2) historic resources; and (3)
affordable housing.

State Election (continued)
QUESTION 1: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
ALLOW LOCAL LICENSING AUTHORITIES TO ISSUE LICENSES FOR FOOD STORES TO SELL WINE
WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
A YES VOTE would create a new category of licenses for food stores to sell wine, and It would allow local licensing authorities to
issue such licenses.
A NO VOTE would make no change in the laws concerning the sale of wine.

QUESTION 2: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
ALLOW A CANDIDATE FOR PUBLIC OFFICE TO BE NOMINATED FOR THE SAME OFFICE BY MORE THAN ONE
POLITICAL PARTY OR DESIGNATION AT THE SAME ELECTION
WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
A YES VOTE would allow a candidate for public office to be nominated for the same office by more than one political party or
political designation at the same election.
A NO VOTE would make no changes in the laws concerning nomination of the candidates for public office.

QUESTION 3: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
ALLOW LICENSED AND OTHER AUTHORIZED PROVIDERS OF CHILD CARE IN PRIVATE HOMES UNDER THE STATE'S
SUBSIDIZED CHILD CARE SYSTEM TO BARGAIN COLLECTIVELY
WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
A YES VOTE would allow licensed and other authorized providers of child care in private homes under the state's subsidized
child care system to bargain collectively with the state.
A NO VOTE would make no change In the laws concerning licensed and other authorized family child care providers.

TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS
The meeting was called to order at 9:13 A.M. in the Lunenburg High School Auditorium by the Town Clerk, she declared the
presence of a quorum and read the call to the meeting. Due to a vacancy in the office of Moderator, the Town Clerk immediately
called for nominations for Temporary Moderator. Attorney Timothy Murphy was nominated, unanimously elected, sworn to the
duties and took up the business of the meeting. The Moderator set out the ground rules for addressing the meeting. The Pledge
of Allegiance to the Flag of the United States of America was led by the Moderator. Retirees Richard Lambert, 33 years with the
Police Department and Barbara Taylor, 15 years with the DPW were recognized for their years of service to the Town of
Lunenburg. Representative Eldridge was recognized and gave an update on the State budget.
SPECIAL TOWN MEETING - May 6, 2006
ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate from available funds all sums of money necessary to amend the
amounts voted for the Town's FY'06 Budget, under Article 4 of the May 7, 2005 Annual Town Meeting Warrant; or take any other
action relative thereto. Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee recommend approval. VOTED UNAMIOUSLY to transfer from
line Item #3/Interest - Temporary Loans $3,000.00, transfer from line Item #13A/Salary Reserve $12,000.00, transfer from line Item
#14/Unemployment Compensation $3,500.00, transfer from line item #16/MacGregor Pension $616.00, transfer from line Item
#19/Selectmen's Administration $5,200.00, transfer from line Item #24A/Treasurer's Administration-Bond Issuance Costs $17,000.00,
transfer from line Item #38/Conservation Commission $8,900.00, transfer from line item #40/Central Purchasing $5,000.00, transfer
from line Item #55/Animal Control $1,500.00, transfer to line Item #12/Public Buildings $5,000.00, transfer to line Item #13/Reserve
Fund $22,118.00, and transfer to line Item #73/Snow removal $19,410.00, to amend the amounts voted for the Town's FY'06 Budget,
under Article 3 of the May 5, 2005 Annual Town Meeting Warrant and Article 1 of the November 15, 2005 Special Town Meeting.
ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to raise and appropriate or transfer from available funds or borrow all sums of money
necessary for drainage rehabilitation in and along Summer Street; or take any other action relative thereto. Finance Committee
recommends approval and Board of Selectmen recommend disapproval. DEFEATED (176 needed for 2/3rds majority Yes 147, No
114) to appropriate $225,000.00.

authorized by Chapter 59 of the General Laws of Massachusetts or any other law is exempt from any surcharge on real property
established under the Act. A taxpayer receiving a regular property tax abatement will also receive a pro rata reduction In
surcharge.

needs and to make annual recommendations to Town Meeting on spending the funds. At least ten (10%) percent of the funds
for each fiscal year will be spent or reserved for later spending on each of the Act's three community preservation purposes; (1)

ARTICLE 36. To see if the town will vote to adopt a by-law establishing a sewer commission as follows: Sewer Commission
(a) Composition, Mode of Selection: - There shall be a sewer commission to consist of five members appointed by the board of
selectmen for terms of one year each.
(b) Powers and Duties: - The sewer commission shall make careful studies of the resources, possibilities and needs of the town
as they relate to the availability of sanitary sewers and shall make plans for the installation of a system of sanitary sewers and for
the maintenance of a sanitary sewer system. The sewer commission shall develop a comprehensive or master plan for a town
wide system of sanitary sewers setting forth in graphic and textual form policies to govern the future growth and development of
the entire town. The sewer commission shall, in conjunction with other land use bodles, develop a long range strategic plan for
guiding town growth and development. The powers and authority of the sewer commission shall Include oversight of the sewer
enterprise fund, to set rates and charges for use of the sanitary sewer system, ratification responsibility for the sewer
superintendent and advice to the board of selectmen relating to Intergovernmental agreements relating to sanitary sewers.
The sewer commission shall make an annual written report to the town meeting, including, among other things, a full accounting
of the Sewer Enterprise Fund, information regarding the condition of the town's sewer system and any plans or proposals for its
development and estimates of their cost.
The sewer commission shall have such other powers, duties and responsibilities as may be provided by by-law.
(c) Appointments: - The sewer commission shall, within thirty days following the submission to it, by the town manager, of the
name of a person to be appointed sewer superintendent, approve or disapprove the appointment of such person. Failure to act
within the said thirty days shall be deemed to be a rejection of the candidate; or take any other action relative thereto.
Submitted by the Charter Revision Committee. Board of Selectmen recommends disapproval. VOTED (Yes 119, No 66) to
adopt a by-law establishing a sewer commission as follows:
Sewer Commission:
(a) Composition, Mode of Selection: - There shall be a sewer commission consisting of five members appointed for terms of three
years each so arranged that the term of office of as nearly an equal number of members as Is possible shall expire each year.
(b) Powers and Duties: - The sewer commission shall make careful studies of the resources, possibilities and needs of the town as
they relate to the availability of sanitary sewers and shall make plans for the Installation of a system of sanitary sewers and for the
maintenance of a sanitary sewer system. The sewer commission shall develop a comprehensive or master plan for a town wide
system, where need Is Identified, of sanitary sewers setting forth in graphic and textual form policies to govern the future growth
and development of the entire town. The sewer commission shall, in conjunction with other land use bodies, develop a long range
strategic plan for guiding town growth and development. The powers and authority of the sewer commission shall Include oversight
of the sewer enterprise fund, and subject to the approval of the Board of Selectmen, to set rates and charges for use of the sanitary
sewer system and advice to the board of selectmen relating to Intergovernmental agreements relating to sanitary sewers.
92
===PAGE 96===
The sewer commission shall make an annual written report to the town meeting, Including, among other things, a full accounting of
the Sewer Enterprise Fund, information regarding the condition of the town's sewer system and any plans or proposal for Its
development and estimates of their cost.
The sewer commission shall have such other powers, dutles and responsibilities as may be provided by by-law.
ARTICLE 37. To see If the town will vote to create a new position of deputy director of finance, to be appointed by the chief
administrative and financial officer and to be in immediate charge of the department of finance.
The deputy director of finance shall, subject to the approval of the chief administrative and financial officer, have the following
powers, duties and responsibilities:
1) Coordinate, administer and supervise all financial services and activities;
2) Assist in all matters related to the financial affairs of the town;
3) Implement and maintain uniform systems, controls and procedures for all financial activities in all departments, including the
school department, and including, but not limited to maintenance and management of all financial and accounting data and
records;
4) Implement and maintain uniform budget guidelines and procedures;
5) Assist in preparation and development of all department budgets and spending plans;
6) Review of all contracts and obligations; monitoring of the expenditure of all funds, including periodic reporting to appropriate
agencies of the status of accounts; establishment of.a spending plan for each town department and spending agency.
In all cases where the duty is not expressly charged to any other department or spending agency, it shall be the duty of the
deputy director of finance to promote, secure and preserve the financial interests of the town.
The deputy director of finance shall from time to time promulgate rules and regulations governing any subject within the
jurisdiction of the department of finance as are necessary or desirable to implement the work of the department of finance.
Upon the effective date of the Revised Charter of the Town of Lunenburg the provisions of this vote shall be deemed repealed
and the provisions of charter section 5-3(b) shall, in all respects, govern the position which shall then be retitled, the director of
municipal finance, or take any other action relative thereto. Submitted by the Charter Revision Committee. Board of
Selectmen recommendation @ Town Meeting, Finance Committee recommends disapproval. ARTICLE 37 was PASSED OVER
ARTICLE 38. To see if the town will vote to petition the General Court for the enactment of a special law revising the Lunenburg
Home Rule Charter adopted at the town election held in 1999, in the following manner
1. Change the designation 'chief administrative and financial officer' to 'town manager' throughout the charter
document.
2. Change the manner of appointment of the finance committee.
3. Change the composition of the town manager selection committee to Include'a member of the finance committee.
4. Change the authority of the Board of Assessors to ratify the appointment of the principal assessor by the town
manager.
5. Change the authority of the Planning board to ratify the appointment of the planning director by the town manager.
6. Expand the power and duties of the deputy moderator.
7. Eliminate the requirement that a town manager must establish a residency in Lunenburg.
8. Change the manner of selection of the Board of Health from a mix of appointed and elected to all elected.
9. Create a partially elected and partially appointed sewer committee.
10. Revise the qualifications for appointment to the office of town manager.
11. Make new provisions for planned and unplanned absences of the town manager.
12. Permit the director of municipal finance to be some one other than the Town Manager.
13. Clarify and confirm the appointment powers of the board of selectmen and the town manager.
14. Make certain other grammatical and phrasing changes that do not alter meaning or intent of existing provisions.
and to authorize the charter review committee to submit to the General Court for enactment as the Revised Charter of the Town

ARTICLE 41. A. No owner or keeper of any dog shall permit such dog, whether licensed or unlicensed, to run at large or to be a
public nuisance within the Town. A dog shall'not, solely by reason of participation in sporting events such as hunting, field trials
or in training-programs, be deemed to be running at large during such period of time as, the dog is actually engaged in the event
or sport If it is under appropriate restraint. Dogs maybe taken outside and to and from the keeper or owner's premises, but only if
the dogs are In restraint.
B. It is unlawful for an owner or keeper of a dangerous dog to permit the dog to be outside an enclosure as described below
unless the dog is muzzled and restrained by a substantial chain or leash and under physical restraint of a responsible person.
The muzzle shall be made in a manner that will not cause injury to the dog or Interfere with its vision or respiration, but shall
prevent it from biting any person or animal. Further, while on the keeper's or owner's property; a dangerous dog shall be
securely confined indoors or in a securely enclosed and locked pen structure, chained or locked to prevent the entry of young
children and designed to prevent the animal from escaping. Such pen or structure shall have secure sides and a secure top, and
shall provide the dog protection from the weather. The owner or keeper shall post notices on the property where the dog is
enclosed. The notices shall be posted at such Intervals and heights and shall be of such size and type SO as to provide clear,
obvious and unambiguous notice that the dog enclosed in the structure is a dangerous dog. The notices shall be understandable
by adults and children.
Fine
1st offense
$50
2nd offense
$100
3rd and subsequent offenses
$300
Submitted by Petitioners. Board of Selectmen & Finance Committee
recommend disapproval. DEFEATED (Yes 50, No 112)

ARTICLE 49. Demolition Delay By-Law Changes:
6.7.2.4.
Significant Building: any building or structure that the Planning Board determines, in conjunction with the
Historical Commission, Building Official and Fire Chief meets one of the following conditions:
a) Listed on the National or State Registries of Historic Places or is the subject of a pending application for
listing of historic places said registries or,
b) Included in a local registry of Historic-Places or Is on a current or pending Inventory prepared by the local
Historic Commission or,
95
===PAGE 99===
c) Importantly associated with one or more historic persons or events, or with the broad architectural,
cultural, political, economic or social history of the Town of Lunenburg or the Commonwealth of
Massachusetts or,
d) Historically or architecturally important in terms to period, style, or method of building construction.
e) Or, aged seventy-five (75) years old or more.
6.7.3
Procedure:
6.7.3.1.
An application for a Demolition Permit will be made to the Building Official who shall within five (5) days forward a
copy of the Demolition Permit application to the Historical Commission. Said application shall include a written
narrative that includes the following:
a) Sketch showing the location of the building or structure with reference to neighboring properties.
b) Photographs
c) Description of the age, architectural style, historical association and importance of the building to be
demolished.
d) Reasons for demolishing.
6.7.3.2.
Demolition Plan Review by the Historical Commission
a) Within thirty (30) days after said application is received by the Historical Commission, the Historical
Commission shall hold a demolition plan review meeting during their next scheduled public meeting (held
on the first Monday of the month, except during the summer months) to determine the applicability of the
delay procedure or the determination of non-applicability of the delay procedure. Said meeting will be
advertised in the local newspaper and posted in the usual manner one (1) week prior to said meeting.
b) Written reports shall be presented by the Historical Commission, Building Official and Fire Chief and the
applicant pertaining to the applicability or non-applicability of delay procedure of the building or structure.
The Historical Commission shall determine the status of the application based on the reports noted
above. The written report of the determination taken shall be given to the Building Official within seven
days (7) of the closing of the review meeting.
c) If the Historic Commission determines the building or structure is not a significant building under this by-
law, the Building Official may Issue a demolition permit upon receipt of the notification.
d) If the building or structure is determined to be a significant building under this bylaw, the Building Official
shall impose a demolition delay up to one hundred eighty (180) days from the date of notification, in
which time the applicant must notify the Historical Commission that a bona fide, reasonable and
unsuccessful effort was made to locate a purchaser to acquire the property at fair market price for the
building or structure and who has agreed to preserve, rehabilitate, restore or relocate same or has
agreed to alternatives to demolition, and or accept conditions noted In the applicability determination.
Submitted by Petitioners. Board of Selectmen recommendation @ Town Meeting. ARTICLE 49 was
PASSED OVER

declared. The ground rules were:reviewed for addressing Town Meeting and we then Pledged Allegiance to the Flag of the
